Abstract: Object removal and semantic inpainting of missing regions remain inherently ill-posed, as multiple plausible completions may exist for a given scene. Recent diffusion-based methods have demonstrated strong potential for high-quality object removal; however, they still face two key limitations. First, although they can synthesise sharp and high-resolution images, they often struggle to complete removed regions in a semantically consistent and contextually meaningful manner, where the generated content should agree with the surrounding scene layout and object relationships rather than merely appear visually realistic. Second, their iterative denoising process makes them computationally expensive, limiting their practicality for downstream applications. We propose SERO, a semantic prediction framework for single-step object removal. Our model predicts a hidden-region semantic prior in embedding space, which guides a latent bridge-matching generator toward plausible scene completion by providing both where to edit and what structure is likely to exist behind the removed object. This enables visually realistic and contextually coherent completions in one forward pass. Experiments on the RORD and OBER datasets show that SERO achieves competitive or state-of-the-art performance while requiring only a single generation step.

</details>

Abstract: Cotton sustains the textile economies of over 80 countries with an annual yield of 113 million bales, yet 30-60% of yield losses are still attributed to viral, bacterial, and fungal diseases that remain undetected until manual field scouting. To address this critical issue, the integration of Unmanned Aerial Vehicle (UAV) imagery with deep learning offers a scalable early-warning system; however, existing models suffer from low early-stage accuracy, poor transferability, and high computational cost. In this paper, we propose Cotton-Vision, a fully convolutional single-stage detector that synergizes three lightweight architectural innovations: Ghost Bottleneck modules for a 20% parameter reduction, Efficient Channel Attention (ECA) for adaptive feature recalibration, and Cross-Stage Partial (CSP) fusion for enriched gradient flow. The network is trained on a novel dataset collected across seven cotton fields in the Punjab province of Pakistan, comprising over 2,700 high-resolution drone images. Extensive data augmentation (rotation, shear, exposure, and saturation) expands the dataset to 11,704 training samples. On a held-out test set, Cotton-Vision achieves a precision of 97.7%, a recall of 77.7%, an mAP@50 of 85.6%, and an mAP@50-95 of 76.6%. These results represent a 10-20% improvement in precision, recall, and mAP over state-of-the-art baselines, including YOLOv5, YOLOv8, YOLOv11, YOLOv12, RT-DETR, and RF-DETR. Through a comprehensive ablation study, we demonstrate that each proposed architectural enhancement contributes to the model's superior performance, yielding a 12-16% mAP improvement over the baseline architecture. Our findings confirm that this synergy of lightweight and attention-based modules provides a robust, computationally viable solution for early cotton disease detection.

Abstract: Proactive image protection methods aim to defend visual content against unauthorized manipulation and generative misuse by embedding protective signals into images before release. Evaluating whether these protections remain effective under image editing is therefore critical. However, existing evaluations remain largely limited to one-shot edits, providing only a static view of robustness while overlooking its evolution under realistic multi-step editing workflows. To systematically characterize robustness dynamics under sequential editing across various protection paradigms, we introduce PREEMPT, a holistic evaluation framework. It evaluates protection robustness under both one-shot and sequential editing scenarios, covering image transformations, semantic edits, homogeneous and heterogeneous edit compositions. To enable consistent analysis across protection paradigms with different underlying objectives, PREEMPT normalizes task-specific effectiveness into protection survival trajectories and summarizes long-horizon robustness through trajectory-based diagnostics. Using this framework, we reveal that degradation behaviors remain hidden under conventional one-shot evaluation. Furthermore, perturbation- and watermark-based protection methods exhibit different failure modes, and the first semantic edit largely determines subsequent degradation trajectories. PREEMPT provides a standardized protocol for studying long-horizon robustness and offers new insights into the degradation dynamics of proactive image protection.

</details>

Abstract: Augmented-reality guidance in laparoscopic liver resection requires aligning preoperative 3D liver models from CT/MRI with intraoperative surfaces. Monocular reconstruction in most cases cannot guarantee scale, albeit rigid-motion registration requires scale as an input. Our proposed framework ``SimCompose'' estimates the full similarity transformation $\Sim$, including scale, without relying on landmarks and applies the same transformation across multiple views with known relative geometry. However, reliable detection of registration failures remains challenging in surgical settings, where ground-truth alignment is generally unavailable. For this, we propose a geometry-based selector that evaluates candidate transformations without requiring ground truth. Large disagreement among these transformations is used to flag potentially unreliable registrations. Finally, we propose to use a non-rigid refinement based on a low-dimensional deformation model from Neo-Hookean simulations in the Simulation Open Framework Architecture (SOFA), without additional network training. SimCompose is trained exclusively on synthetic partial views. Its encoder processes point clouds normalised to a unit root-mean-square radius, without access to metric scale, and filters each view before multi-view fusion. Evaluation on unseen synthetic data (300 single-view pairs per run; 100 three-view registrations) shows that multi-view integration and candidate selection increase the success rate under stringent $\Sim$ accuracy criteria from 38.4% for a single-view, single-method configuration to 77%. Existing learning-based baselines do not satisfy these criteria even when provided with the exact scale. On five in-vivo patients with 92 keyframes, the rigid registration stage reduces surface error more than three-fold relative to learning-based baselines with external scale information.

</details>

Abstract: While the Pepper's Ghost illusion is a 19th-century optical technique, we found that this forgotten gimmick exposes vulnerabilities in modern face recognition systems. This paper introduces the 'Ghost Attack': a physical attack that repurposes this classic illusion using low-cost, free-space hardware and a transparent medium to project high-resolution facial data into physical 3D space. We show in the paper that despite relying on centuries-old optical principles, this new attack type simultaneously breaks three components of modern facial recognition systems (FRS): (a) the face matcher, (b) the presentation attack detection module, and (c) the morphing attack detection component, resulting in high impersonation success rates against state-of-the-art algorithms without triggering presentation or morphing defenses. These findings reveal a significant blind spot in current biometric systems, which fail to account for transparent projections and evolving physical attack vectors. To support further research, we are offering the dataset of face images of 100 identities, re-captured with and without 'ghost' projections as a new benchmark representing a novel attack for face recognition.

Abstract: Text-conditioned 3D human--object interaction (HOI) generation remains challenging because individually plausible human and object motions do not necessarily form a coherent interaction. We argue that a key limitation lies in conventional skeleton-based representations, which compactly describe body kinematics while leaving surface-level interaction geometry largely implicit, often requiring additional contact-specific regularizers. We propose ULIFlow, a framework that explicitly models continuous human--object relations within a unified latent space. To this end, we derive continuous marker--object relations from sparse body-surface markers and treat them as an explicit interaction modality alongside human motion and object dynamics. A Mixture-of-Transformers (MoT) autoencoder is then designed to jointly model these heterogeneous streams while preserving their modality-specific structures. Importantly, we let the relation stream directly participate in human and object reconstruction, encouraging the shared latent representation to preserve both individual dynamics and the interaction geometry that coordinates them. To further achieve variable-length generation, we introduce a blockwise Flow Forcing scheme with global sequence-progress conditioning, enabling progressive continuation from fixed-length training windows while maintaining consistent long-horizon dynamics. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ULIFlow consistently outperforms prior work in overall generation quality and human--object coordination, while preserving coherent interactions over requested durations beyond the training horizon.

Abstract: Low-light video enhancement requires the recovery of brightness, color, and fine spatial structures while preserving temporal coherence in the presence of noise and motion. Existing methods often struggle to balance these objectives, particularly in severely degraded regions. This paper presents CRISP, a recurrent framework trained using a progressive illumination curriculum. Its two-frame recurrent architecture propagates information across successive frames to exploit temporal context beyond the immediate input pair. During training, supervision progressively transitions from intermediate targets interpolated between the low-light input and the reference to the full reference, gradually increasing the required brightness and color correction. This curriculum stabilizes recurrent training without introducing additional inference cost and yields the largest performance improvement among the components evaluated in the ablation study. To further support structural reconstruction, the framework optionally incorporates frozen DINOv3 semantic features through adaptive layer normalization (AdaLN)-style modulation, without fine-tuning the foundation model. Experiments on DID and BVI-RLV demonstrate that CRISP achieves the highest PSNR on DID and the highest SSIM on BVI-RLV among the evaluated methods, while maintaining computational efficiency.

Abstract: Image representation affects both the forward model and quantitative feature recovery in model-based iterative CT reconstruction. For isotropically bandlimited signals, the hexagonal lattice is the most sampling-efficient lattice in two dimensions, with greater directional isotropy and a larger alias-free bandwidth than the Cartesian grid at equal density. We develop constant and linear hexagonal projectors for two-dimensional fan-beam CT using box spline calculus. Both bases form a partition of unity on common coefficient centers; the linear tent also reproduces affine functions. We derive analytic ray footprints and effective detector-blur expressions and implement efficient GPU forward and backprojection. On simulated FORBILD data, the linear model improves on Cartesian and hexagonal pixels, with its best noisy-data accuracy at the coarser resolution. Real-world 2DeteCT comparisons use high-dose, high-resolution Cartesian reconstructions as pseudo ground truth and show competitive image and feature agreement. These results establish a practical hexagonal representation for quantitative CT.

To address these challenges, we propose PACE-Mem, Prefix-Aware Complementary Evidence, which constructs a complementary evidence sequence and determines its length from the same selection trajectory. For structured memories, query-guided pooling combines records within each clip to obtain relevance scores and representations. Using these relevance scores and representations, maximal marginal relevance (MMR) selection balances query relevance against overlap with previously selected evidence. From the resulting score trajectory, a prefix-length selection algorithm determines retrieval depth by comparing length-normalized adjacent declines, accounting for both score changes and the amount of evidence accumulated. PACE-Mem is a training-free, plug-in retrieval method for clip-based agent memory systems, leaving memory construction and answer generation unchanged. Across three long-video benchmarks and two agent frameworks, PACE-Mem improves question-answering accuracy by an average of 1.6% over Adaptive-k, the strongest baseline at comparable mean retrieval depths. Further analysis reveals that jointly adding and removing evidence is associated with larger net accuracy gains than either change alone.

Abstract: Deep neural networks can achieve strong predictive performance in medical imaging while relying on regions unrelated to the predicted finding. Abstract: We present Spatons, a family of compositional, spatially localized neural operators for computationally efficient XR (extended reality) predistortion and distortion compensation. XR displays (AR, VR, and MR) suffer from spatial (e.g., geometric) and tonal (e.g., colour) distortions. Frames can be pre-distorted so that the projected image looks as intended. Distortions vary with manufacturing tolerances and projection systems, motivating programmable compensation. Untethered, battery-operated XR systems also require low energy and latency. A Spaton is a hybrid patch-to-pixel block combining tiny Multi-Layer Perceptrons (MLPs) with fixed-function interpolation. Using Spatons, we build programmable, computationally efficient pipelines for varied distortion scenarios. On the full spatiotonal Scenario 5, Sequential-5' matches CompenNet++ in SSIM (0.944 vs. 0.944) and closely matches its PSNR (27.5 vs. 27.7 dB), with substantially lower complexity. We demonstrate a flexible Spaton solution with 6 times fewer FLOPs and 106 times fewer parameters than CompenNet++, and 4-10 times fewer memory transfers than UNet++. We characterize software implementations and analytical hardware estimates, including optimizations across target XR platforms. The distortions considered are drawn mostly from see-through, near-eye displays, where the spatial light path governs local intensity and spectral response. This early systems-level exploration uses closed-form differentiable distortions and one real projector-camera capture set for training and evaluation, rather than through-the-optics measurements. Testing on a wider range of real XR devices remains future work. Chromatic dispersion (color fringes) remains the main artifact where Spatons trail monolithic baselines.

In Phase I, a segmenter is trained using confidence-weighted supervision and dual-scale consistency. In Phase II, model feedback is used to adaptively target underperforming categories and refine diffusion-derived masks using model predictions. Abstract: We introduce avatar referring segmentation, which selects regions of animatable Gaussian avatars from language queries. A query can also specify a pose or motion to select matching intervals. This lets users interact with avatars for local editing and motion inspection. However, making these selections with existing language Gaussian splatting methods poses two challenges: adjacent parts and garments are hard to separate and may be referred to indirectly (region ambiguity), while fine-grained motions can look alike in RGB (motion ambiguity). We address these challenges with HumanSkill-4D, a training-free framework that shares a multimodal large language model (MLLM) and geometric operators across avatars. For each avatar, a semantic memory links body-part and garment categories to persistent Gaussian regions and appearance descriptions. The MLLM uses category names and descriptions to select categories by appearance, relation, or function. To find matching intervals, it composes operators into programs that check query conditions on driving joint trajectories stored in a motion memory. These category choices and operator outputs make selections traceable, while composing or adding operators supports new motion conditions. To evaluate this task, we introduce three human-annotated benchmarks and show that HumanSkill-4D outperforms the baselines in part segmentation and 3D and 4D referring segmentation, with compact memories and only minutes of setup per avatar.

Abstract: Online scene change detection requires an agent to detect what has changed in a previously mapped environment while it is still on site, observing from unconstrained viewpoints and without access to future observations. This causal setting is strictly harder than its offline counterpart, and online methods have consistently trailed offline accuracy. O-SCD closed that gap by treating change as a persistent property of a 3D Gaussian splatting map of the reference scene, surpassing the strongest offline methods in change detection accuracy while operating at over 10 frames per second. O-SCD optimizes a change field over the Gaussians of the reference map, refitting it to all past cue maps at every frame, a step that consumes more than half of the per-frame budget and forces accuracy to be traded against speed. We present a replay-free formulation that eliminates this trade-off. Because the reference map stays fixed, rendering the change field is a linear operation. Each new observation can therefore be carried onto the map's Gaussians directly, by a single backward pass of the renderer, which reduces multi-view fusion to an exact two-scalar recursion per Gaussian. Past observations are never stored or revisited. The self-supervised objective that O-SCD minimizes by descent also determines the decision threshold exactly, by sorting, yielding a label-free per-frame cutoff whose margin provides a selective-prediction signal. On PASLCD, our method improves mean intersection over union from 0.489 to 0.575 and throughput from 11.8 to 25.6 frames per second, even exceeding O-SCD's offline result while remaining strictly causal.

</details>

Abstract: Frequency-domain methods have shown strong potential for low-light image enhancement (LLIE) because they can effectively regulate global illumination. However, most existing methods assume that amplitude and phase can be restored independently, while overlooking their inherent spectral coupling. This artificial decoupling often leads to information inconsistency and structural distortions. To address this issue, we propose a frequency-spatial framework that jointly models spectral interaction and reliable prior guidance. We first empirically analyze the coupling between amplitude and phase and develop an Amplitude-Phase Adaptive Compensation Fusion (APACF) module. Instead of enforcing a hard separation between the two components, APACF enables their bidirectional interaction, resulting in spectrally coherent restoration. In addition, directly extracting priors from raw low-light inputs is unreliable, since severe noise and underexposure destroy critical visual content. To alleviate this problem, we introduce an Endogenous Reliability Prior (ERP). Specifically, we treat an intermediate result, preliminarily restored in the frequency domain and already close to the ground-truth illumination, as a high-fidelity source for extracting complementary pseudo-infrared and gradient cues. Based on ERP, we further design a Dual-Modal Guided Refinement Network (DGRN) to inject these purified structural cues into the decoder, thereby recovering missing fine textures more accurately. Extensive experiments on benchmark datasets show that our method achieves state-of-the-art performance and consistently surpasses existing methods in both quantitative metrics and visual quality. Our code is available at https://github.com/bywlzts/Amplitude-Phase-Coupling-Net.

Abstract: As AI-assisted image editing becomes increasingly prevalent, Text-Centric Image Forgery Localization (TFL) is essential for protecting trust in financial and legal records. However, we observe that existing TFL detectors predominantly rely on Full-Parameter Fine-Tuning (FPFT) and fail to generalize to unseen forgery types because FPFT can induce low-rank feature collapse and overfit training artifacts. Meanwhile, prior detectors capture diverse forgery traces with a single unified representation, making subtle traces entangled and difficult to learn. To address these challenges, we propose RiSE, a Residual Subspace Expert model that preserves pre-trained priors by freezing principal SVD components and adapting only the residual subspaces with a ViT-only segmentor to avoid feature collapse. RiSE introduces forgery-aware residual experts, where each expert is trained independently on a training subset with a particular forgery type, and we show that cross-domain localization performance improves consistently as more experts are added. At inference, we introduce Latent Perturbation Confidence (LPC), which selects the most confident expert by analytically computing the latent-perturbed output. LPC enables efficient confidence estimation with a single forward pass, avoiding repeated forward passes on perturbed samples and improving expert selection. By training on synthetic forgery data, RiSE with 1 expert outperforms prior methods on real-world forgery images by 20.4% while reducing training steps by 10 times. Scaling the number of experts to 9 yields a 35.8% gain. RiSE remains effective with DCT feature, even when the majority of the parameters is frozen with RGB-only pretraining. Abstract: Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) demonstrate strong performance on multiple-choice question answering (MCQA), yet their reliability under biased conditions remains underexplored. We investigate length bias, the systematic preference for longer answer options, through controlled benchmarks spanning fine-grained visual domains. LVLMs consistently favor longer options even when visually inconsistent, with accuracy dropping 2--38\% when correct answers are shorter and increasing 1--70\% when longer. This bias persists across model scales. Through layer-wise mechanistic analysis, we reveal that length bias emerges from late-layer suppression of visual evidence in favor of language-side length priors. Blurry-image experiments show that bias intensifies when visual evidence weakens, while abstention tests reveal that LVLMs struggle to refuse incorrect predictions. We propose and evaluate multiple inference-time mitigation strategies; while these provide partial improvements, none achieves deployable bias mitigation without relying on oracle information unavailable at inference time. These results underscore that inference-time interventions are insufficient, pointing to the need for architectural solutions and bias-aware training.

Abstract: Best-of-N methods improve text-only LLM reasoning by sampling multiple trajectories before selecting an answer. For Vision-Language Models (VLMs), answer frequency and model confidence alone can be insufficient, as sampled generations also vary in their engagement with the visual input. We find that distributional confidence and visual attention are complementary indicators of rollout quality. Each is limited on its own, while trajectories that show strong signals in both are more likely to be correct. Motivated by this, we introduce VisConf, a rollout-quality score that multiplicatively combines visual engagement with candidate-calibrated Self-Certainty, penalizing rollouts that are weak in either signal. We incorporate Rank-Weighted Consensus (RWC), a tuning-free aggregation rule that converts within-question VisConf ranks into answer-level vote weights. Across three VLMs on MathVista, MMMU-Pro, and MMStar, VisConf with RWC consistently outperforms strong BoN baselines. Our analysis shows particularly large gains when correct trajectories are present but underrepresented in the candidate pool.

Abstract: Adverse weather conditions such as fog, rain, snow, and low illumination introduce structured visibility degradations that severely impair object detectors trained on clear-weather data. When source data are inaccessible due to privacy or trans- mission constraints, source-free domain adaptation (SFDA) becomes necessary. However, existing SFDA methods based on pairwise contrastive learning struggle to capture high-order semantics and often suffer from agreement collapse under severe corruption. We propose REUNION, a hypergraph-guided SFDA framework for object detection in adverse weather conditions. REUNION constructs a contrastive hypergraph over target-domain object proposals, encoding high-order relations through intra-image context, weather-aware grouping, prototype-based semantic anchors, and uncertainty-aware connections between reliable and low-confidence instances. To effectively exploit these structured relations, we introduce a group-wise Hyper-InfoNCE objective that optimizes representations at the hyperedge level, enabling semantic information to propagate from confident proposals to corrupted or low-contrast instances. Experiments with diverse benchmarks demonstrate that REUNION effectively mitigates domain shifts and achieves state-of-the-art performance in SFDA settings.

Abstract: Long videos often exceed the context capacity of multimodal large language models (MLLMs), while agentic search repeats costly video processing and grounding for each query. Reusable memory can amortize this cost, but must preserve information for questions unknown during construction. This representation combines compact free-form text with sparse images to preserve semantic content and complementary visual details across temporal scales, supporting temporal navigation without a fixed semantic schema. With the underlying models frozen, we evolve their workflows and operators using distinct feedback: query-independent source fidelity and coverage for the Writer, and QA failures in evidence localization and utilization for the Reader. Abstract: Few-shot classifiers of rock thin sections are meta-trained on many rock types that a petrographer has identified slide by slide, and this identification is the costly step that new collections lack. We study few-shot recognition of unseen rock types when no training slide is identified, and the only information available is what the microscope records anyway: which slide an image comes from, and whether it was taken in plane-polarized (PPL) or cross-polarized (XPL) light. We first show why generic unsupervised pseudo-tasks fit this setting poorly: the positives they find without labels are either wrong or near-duplicates of the anchor, so they teach the model little. Polarized-light microscopy provides positives that avoid both problems. The PPL and XPL views of one field show the same grains, so they are correct by construction, yet they look very different. We propose cross-polarization pairing (XPP), which treats each slide as a pseudo-class and pairs its PPL and XPL views of the same field location, so that matching must rely on grain shape and texture rather than on colour. On the Nanjing University collection of 108 rock types, with support and query images taken from different slides, XPP is the best label-free method on average over three random splits, and matches a supervised model trained on 78 identified rock types. Controls show that colour augmentation and other acquisition positives do not reproduce the gain. We also show that the image-level protocol of earlier work is saturated, and propose a cross-slide protocol in its place.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models offer a promising paradigm for autonomous driving. However, the massive parameter scale of VLAs strains onboard computational resources during real-time inference. This directly increases energy consumption and limits vehicle battery range, making it critical to minimize computational overhead without compromising driving capability and explainability. Current research on efficient VLA-based driving focuses on sequential adaptation (\textit{e.g.}, Chain-of-Thought and token adjustment), leaving structural adaptation underexplored despite parameter redundancy across dynamic driving scenes. To address this gap, we propose Scene-Adaptive VLA, an efficient framework leveraging dynamic layer routing to adjust active parameters based on real-time scenes. Inspired by the temporal continuity of scenes and model predictive uncertainty, our approach characterizes model-perceived scene complexity via frame-level temporal scene variations and decision deviations. To uncover latent scene-to-parameter correspondences, we encode this characterization into scene-aware tokens, alongside two specialized queries. A budget-conditioned layer routing mechanism evaluates these refined queries to allocate a budget that determines the active parameter ratio and subsequently selects appropriate layer combinations. Abstract: Vision Transformer (ViT) has garnered increased attention for image classification in recent years. However, ViT is notorious for its data-hungry problem as it achieves high-performance results when only trained on large-size datasets. In this paper, we study how ViT specifically works on small-scale datasets with limited data where the availability of image data is often constrained in practice, especially in fields such as medical imaging. To mitigate the issue, we designed a multi-stage residual ViT, dubbed ReSEViT, with the self-ensemble method to utilize multi-level information from intermediate features. Due to the complex decision boundaries for classifications, relying on a single classifier can lead to prediction errors. Hence, we propose a new learning method for ViT, named self-ensemble learning, which ensembles multi-stage classifiers in a single model. Our self-ensemble comprises a novel approach, Dynamic Learner Selection (DLS), to adaptively select the importance of different stages. ReSEViT incorporates residual token embeddings which is a ResNet-like block to model effective local structures alongside with transformer blocks in each stage. The integration of residual tokens and self-ensemble learning into a multi-stage ViT highlights the reduction in redundancy, prediction error and variance while maintaining an efficient model size for small-scale image datasets.

Abstract: Recovering metric 3D geometry from images is fundamentally ill-posed due to perspective projection ambiguity. Current geometry models incorporate supplied or predicted intrinsics during inference, but do not use them to supervise model adaptation. To refine these predictions, test-time adaptation (TTA) typically relies on external depth measurements. In this paper, we instead use camera intrinsics as an output-side geometric verifier. Comparing the point cloud's viewing rays with those defined by the known intrinsics yields a direct measure of geometric inconsistency. We propose \textit{IntriAdapt}, a TTA framework that minimizes this closed-form geometric residual. By optimizing a lightweight LoRA adapter on a frozen geometry foundation model, our method aligns the prediction with the specific camera model without depth sensors or external priors. We further extend this approach to video sequences, demonstrating that adapting a short sequence generalizes to other sequences captured by the same camera. The adapted model processes held-out images in a feed-forward pass, without repeating optimization for each frame. Experiments on ten single-image and seven sequence benchmarks across multiple base models show that \textit{IntriAdapt} consistently outperforms both unadapted and oracle-intrinsics baselines in metric accuracy and relative geometry.

</details>

Although diffusion-based MOT provides an effective iterative proposal refinement paradigm, existing diffusion-based trackers lack dedicated designs for exploiting multispectral cues in proposal initialization and confidence calibration, limiting tracking robustness in complex UAV scenarios. This letter proposes DSATrack, a diffusion-guided spectral-aware tracking framework for multispectral UAV MOT. Specifically, a lightweight spectral encoder injects multispectral information into the RPN branch through feature fusion, enabling the RPN head to generate high-quality object-aware proposals. These proposals are then injected into the initial proposal set to provide reliable spatial priors for diffusion refinement. Furthermore, a spectral-saliency confidence modulation strategy recalibrates refined tracking confidence according to local multispectral saliency contrast before score thresholding, enhancing spectrally reliable candidates while suppressing unreliable ones. Without modifying the original diffusion refinement head, DSATrack improves both proposal quality and post-refinement candidate selection. Experiments on the MMOT benchmark demonstrate that DSATrack outperforms the adapted DiffusionTrack baseline by 4.6 HOTA, 9.0 MOTA, and 7.7 IDF1 points, showing its effectiveness for multispectral UAV MOT.

Abstract: We study the problem of mapping parameter-space variance of a 3D Morphable Model (3DMM) to per-vertex variance. Traditionally, per-vertex variance has been used to visually interpret the learnt parameter uncertainty. However, mapping parameter-space variance to per-vertex using sampling or error propagation is computationally expensive. We address this limitation with Closed-form Local Uncertainty Estimation (CLUE), which transforms the parameter-space distribution of 3DMMs to per-vertex through a closed-form approximation. CLUE emerges from applying the law of total variance to the 3DMM formulation and is modular, computationally inexpensive, and accurate. Subsequently, we study integrating CLUE for in-the-wild human mesh recovery training and inference. We note that CLUE uses the geometry of the mesh as an inductive bias when predicting the variance, leading to improved accuracy. Through extensive qualitative and quantitative experiments, we show that when used in training, CLUE (1) outpeforms probabilistic and deterministic baselines on metrics evaluating joints, mesh and uncertainty, and (2) has the highest uncertainty-to-error spearman correlation, with applications including data mining and interpretable uncertainty. Finally, we share SAM 3D Body (CLUE) which augments the regressor with high resolution uncertainty maps. We make our code available at https://github.com/vita-epfl/CLUE

Abstract: Large-scale pre-trained models have become foundational to modern machine learning, yet conventional pre-training objectives primarily optimize source-domain performance without explicitly considering downstream requirements. Consequently, transferable and task-specific knowledge remains entangled within a shared parameter space, limiting selective knowledge reuse and efficient downstream adaptation. This limitation is particularly consequential for image-generation models, which must accommodate diverse conditions and domains. To address this challenge, we introduce Knowledge Diversion (KDiv), a condition-aware pre-training paradigm for learning inherently decomposable generative models. Before pre-training, KDiv factorizes randomly initialized weights into rank-one Knowledge Units, which are organized into learngenes and tailors. During pre-training, continuous condition-aware routing consolidates knowledge shared across conditions in learngenes while directing condition-dependent variations to tailors. This structured organization enables learngenes to capture transferable generative priors and tailors to develop condition-specific expertise, allowing the resulting model to be selectively recombined for diverse downstream conditions. Extensive experiments across class-, text-, and image-conditioned generation demonstrate that KDiv consistently matches or surpasses dense baselines at comparable pre-training cost and achieves the strongest average downstream performance with a trainable parameter budget comparable to low-rank adaptation methods.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) have achieved remarkable progress in 2D visual understanding tasks, yet the passive view-centric perception paradigm limits their capacity for 3D spatial reasoning. Such reasoning often requires actively acquiring spatial evidence beyond the currently visible views, such as camera poses and cross-view spatial relationships, to infer 3D relationships that are not explicitly represented in 2D images. However, existing methods either lack active exploration or merely provide additional views during inference, without explicitly acquiring such spatial evidence beyond 2D images. In this paper, we propose View-to-World (V2W), an active exploration framework that enables VLMs to actively explore 3D worlds and lifts VLMs from passive view-centric perception to active world-centric spatial reasoning. Given a spatial reasoning task with multi-view images, V2W first reconstructs an explicit 3D world with geometry, camera poses, and language-grounded semantics, and exposes it to VLMs through visual and linguistic interfaces. Through multi-turn interaction with these interfaces, VLMs iteratively acquire spatial evidence from the constructed 3D world, enabling active world-centric spatial reasoning. V2W can be integrated with existing VLMs in a training-free manner and can be further enhanced by optimizing the exploration policy via reinforcement learning. Experiments on spatial mental modeling benchmarks demonstrate that V2W substantially improves VLM baselines in the training-free setting and achieves state-of-the-art performance with the learned exploration policy, highlighting the importance of active exploration for spatial intelligence. Abstract: Despite the significant progress in image enhancement under adverse illuminations, such as low-light image enhancement, exposure correction, and backlit image enhancement, existing models show limited generalizability to different scenarios. A major bottleneck lies in the lack of large-scale and diverse training data, as these tasks typically rely on manually captured, precisely aligned image pairs that are expensive to scale up. Although synthetic data have been explored as an alternative, existing synthesis methods are limited in modeling complex adverse illumination conditions and camera imaging pipelines. To address this issue, we propose a novel framework to synthesize realistic training pairs for generalizable image enhancement in the wild. Specifically, we first derive multi-exposure sequences (MES) from high-bit RAW images by rendering them under different exposure settings through an emulated ISP pipeline. Using these RAW-derived MES as supervision, we train a diffusion-based RGB-to-MES generator to synthesize plausible exposure sequences from a single 8-bit RGB image, extending the synthesis pipeline from limited RAW collections to large-scale in-the-wild RGB data. For each source image, we randomly sample one synthesized exposure variant as the degraded input and use the original high-quality RGB image as the target. With the proposed MES Synthesis (MESS) approach, we construct a dataset of 500K training pairs and train a lightweight network, which demonstrates significantly better generalization performance than existing models across various image enhancement tasks. Abstract: Recovering missing visual information from incomplete or degraded images remains a challenging problem, particularly when spatial variations and complex image structures affect reconstruction accuracy. Although Spatial Transformer Networks (STNs) facilitate spatial feature alignment, their reliance on conventional linear or bilinear interpolation limits their ability to capture the underlying geometric characteristics of image data. Furthermore, interpolation based on a restricted set of neighbouring pixels can introduce inconsistencies in gradient propagation, potentially affecting optimisation stability. To address these limitations, we propose a Geometry-Aware Interpolation Network (GAIN), a variational autoencoder-based framework that incorporates manifold geometry into spatial transformation and image reconstruction. Rather than estimating pixel intensities solely from fixed spatial neighbours, GAIN exploits the local tangent structure of the data manifold to construct geometry-informed intensity approximations. An entropy-based regularisation mechanism further guides the estimation of spatial transformation parameters, promoting more adaptive interpolation. In addition, a convolutional normalisation strategy is introduced to regulate the magnitude of non-zero activations and improve gradient stability during training. Extensive experiments on challenging image completion and classification benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of GAIN in improving predictive accuracy while reducing computational cost compared with existing methods.

</details>

Abstract: High-quality image editing requires precise modification of target regions while preserving the content of unrelated regions. Existing editing models produce flattened outputs that limit precise local manipulation and layer reuse, while decomposition methods typically optimize layer recovery separately from instruction-driven editing. To this end, we introduce LayerEditor, a unified framework that couples instruction-guided image editing with controllable generation of reusable RGBA layers. At its core, In-Context RGB-Alpha Token Modeling jointly represents the edited canvas and an ordered sequence of RGB-Alpha layers within a single token sequence, enabling semantic edits and explicit control over individual layer content. To retain the pretrained model's editing capability during adaptation to layered outputs, we propose Compositional Edit-Prior Distillation, which transfers its editing prior to both the edited canvas and the image composed from the predicted layers. To support training and evaluation, we construct LayerEditor-100K, a large-scale paired image-editing dataset with ordered RGBA layer annotations, and introduce dedicated benchmarks for layered editing. Extensive experiments demonstrate that LayerEditor maintains strong editing fidelity while generating accurate RGBA layers, supporting both flexible layer-level manipulation and multi-layer decomposition.

Abstract: Flow-matching models generate samples through iterative velocity predictions, but typically do not revisit these predictions using the intermediate representations that produced them. We introduce CorrFlow, a self-correcting flow-matching framework that reuses this information through a lightweight refiner. Under rectified-flow interpolation, the error of the one-step clean prediction equals the realized velocity residual scaled by the noise level. Motivated by this identity, the refiner learns to predict residual corrections from the base model's clean prediction, conditioning, noise level and hidden states from the same forward pass. The correction is added to the base velocity before each sampling update, so subsequent predictions are made from the corrected state. This requires no additional base-model evaluations and only one lightweight refiner evaluation per step. Our theoretical analyses establish conditions under which feature reuse outperforms raw-input affine predictors and refiner training achieves greater residual-risk reduction than continued base-model training under a matched training compute budget. Experiments on class-conditional image generation, text-to-video generation and image-to-video generation show consistent improvements over the corresponding base models, including gains in visual quality and physical consistency. Project Page: https://fogradio.github.io/corrflow-project

Abstract: Open-vocabulary semantic segmentation aims to classify pixels without being restricted to a fixed set of categories. Although Contrastive Language-Image Pre-training (CLIP) demonstrates remarkable potential for open-vocabulary recognition, its segmentation capability is impaired by noisy inter-class patch interactions. To this end, this work proposes a unified training-free object-centric framework, named CorrCLIPv2, from three complementary aspects of patch correlation, feature representation, and output regularization. Specifically, for patch correlation, we leverage class-agnostic mask generators to explicitly disentangle inter-class interactions. Moreover, we utilize spatially-coherent features from self-supervised models to compute attention scores to mitigate mask inaccuracies. Regarding feature representation, we devise a refinement mechanism to simultaneously strengthen spatial details and semantic representation. Within the output space, we unify predictions via voting in each mask to enhance spatial consistency. To mitigate contextual bias and enhance prediction accuracy of objects, we further extend the framework by incorporating a cross-image object retrieval paradigm, which incurs minimal computational overhead. We build a memory bank of CorrCLIP-enhanced features from unlabeled images and implement a regime of multi-CLIP ensemble. By aggregating semantically consistent object features across diverse contexts retrieved from the memory bank, CorrCLIPv2 mitigates context-induced interference and obtains more robust predictions. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our approach establishes a new state-of-the-art on 10 standard benchmarks. Abstract: As multimodal resources are transformed into media outputs, agentic AI is expected to stay aligned with design intent over long horizons. Designers follow briefs specifying goals, constraints, and deliverables, with later work building on earlier decisions. Recent work reuses design experience across tasks, yet alignment among artifacts within one project remains challenging. Agents therefore need to preserve and apply this intent across domains and as projects grow. We introduce Design Anything, a graph-based harness that preserves design intent outside the model and binds deliverables to the workflow steps that produce them through an intent-execution graph. A task contract retains constraints and decisions, selected designs become explicit inputs to later generations, and a deterministic controller uses bindings and execution records to check output types and counts, identify outstanding deliverables, and guide subsequent turns. We also introduce DesignAnything-Bench, with 228 tasks across 19 design domains, including 38 project families at scales of 6, 14, and 24 artifacts. With the LLM held fixed, Design Anything satisfies 85.0% and 92.5% of the brief's factual constraints with DeepSeek V4.1 Flash High and GPT-5.6 Sol High, respectively, exceeding the strongest same-model baselines by 15.4% and 14.4%. These gains reach 25.1% and 23.0% on 24-artifact tasks. Under each model, Design Anything leads or ties in constraint satisfaction in 18 of 19 domains. Professional designers also prefer its complete deliverable sets over those of same-model baselines in a blind evaluation.

Abstract: Reasoning Video Object Segmentation (RVOS) aims to localize and segment objects referred to by natural-language expressions throughout a video. Although large vision-language models (LVLMs) implicitly encode localization cues in their attention maps, these attentions are often noisy, diffuse, and scattered across layers, heads, and modalities, limiting their direct use for segmentation. Abstract: Comparing videos to assess their semantic similarity remains a challenging task for current Large Multimodal Models (LMMs), which are primarily trained with single-video objectives. While most prior works have focused on computing global similarity, recent studies have introduced the more challenging problem of concept-based similarity, which measures how similar two videos are with respect to specific concepts (e.g., activity, location). However, no dedicated approaches have yet leveraged LMMs to explicitly address this task. In this paper, we aim to fill this gap introducing Video Caption Entailment for video similarity estimation (VICE): a dual-branch design that leverages LMMs for cross-video reasoning. Each branch jointly processes one video and the caption of the other, measuring their alignment for a specific concept via video-language entailment. The outputs from both branches are fused to produce a final similarity score, along with a textual explanation of the model reasoning. This approach leverages the model's native single-video understanding while enabling fine-grained comparative reasoning between video and text. Experiments on ConViS-Bench and StepDiff show that VICE outperforms models that process both videos simultaneously, highlighting the promise of single-video reasoning for concept-based similarity estimation.

Abstract: Many real-world capture scenarios involve an active light source rigidly attached to the camera: camera flashes for dark-environment imaging, underwater vehicle lights, and endoscopic/laparoscopic medical procedures. As the camera moves, the co-mounted light moves with it, causing the illumination direction at each surface point to change across views. Neural rendering methods (NeRF, Gaussian Splatting) encode this pose-dependent illumination as surface coloration, producing appearance inconsistencies and making it harder to recover the underlying scene. We present \textbf{DaFISH}, which can be plugged into any novel-view synthesis (NVS) pipeline such as NeRF. It factorizes per-point emitted color into surface albedo and a spherical harmonic (SH)-represented illumination factor. During training, the SH illumination is evaluated directly, enabling stable joint optimization of albedo, lighting, and geometry. \textbf{DaFISH} provides analytical generalization of illumination to arbitrary camera poses without retraining. Building on the closed-form SH-to-irradiance conversion, the training pipeline is fully differentiable from SH evaluation through volume rendering. Averaged over the held-out frames of the five scenes, \textbf{DaFISH} attains the best mean PSNR (38.91\,dB), SSIM (0.9613), and LPIPS (0.0582) among all compared methods. It improves over the strongest splatting baseline as well as the closest NeRF-based baseline, while recovering the scene consistently across views compared to prior methods.

Abstract: 3D Gaussian Splatting has made high-fidelity 3D reconstruction fast and accessible, yet existing representations assume a static world. This assumption breaks down in casual captures, where a user naturally interacts with the environment -- opening doors, toggling switches, or moving objects -- introducing discrete state changes that no current framework can model. Time-conditioned dynamic methods such as 4DGS conflate when a state was observed with what state the scene is in, limiting them to replaying seen sequences rather than composing arbitrary object configurations. We present SwitchSplat, the first 3DGS representation to address this gap via a base+slot decomposition: a shared static base captures the invariant scene, while each slot independently holds the two binary states (on/off) of one interactable object. Because the assignment of Gaussians to slots is fully differentiable, the model discovers the geometry of each state directly from video, without manual annotation. At inference, the user can freely combine slot states and render the resulting scene from any viewpoint. We validate SwitchSplat on a new casual-capture dataset and show that it outperforms static and dynamic baselines in novel-view synthesis quality, while uniquely supporting rendering of state combinations never seen during capture. Project page: https://switchsplat.github.io

Instead of scoring queries against fragmented token evidence, SL-Ground encodes each category description as a complete sentence-level representation and directly scores object queries against these representations. To evaluate fine-grained recognition across label granularity, we construct a multi-granularity Stanford Cars benchmark spanning vehicle type, brand, and car model. At the finest car-model granularity, SL-Ground raises AP over the token-level variant from 14.5 to 70.6 under matched training candidate sampling and to 86.0 with full-vocabulary training, while better preserving open-vocabulary capability and avoiding repeated prompt chunking. Beyond Stanford Cars, SL-Ground also improves FG-OVD performance, achieving the best average AP among compared methods. These results show that sentence-level grounding provides a more suitable scoring unit for fine-grained OVOD and offers a practical path toward fine-grained grounding-based open-vocabulary detectors.

Abstract: Reducing MRI acquisition time improves patient comfort, reduces motion artefacts, and increases scanner throughput. Super-resolution can recover higher-resolution images, but existing methods are typically specialised to one scale or acquisition geometry, limiting their use across heterogeneous clinical protocols. We present GSMR3D, a continuous volumetric model that reconstructs arbitrary isotropic and anisotropic resolutions with a single checkpoint per MRI contrast. It combines a learned dictionary of anisotropic 3D Gaussian prototypes with scale-conditioned frequency encoding and an exact measurement-consistency projection, keeping reconstructions consistent with the acquired low-resolution data. We train separate models on 3,731 contrast-enhanced T1-weighted and 30,254 T1-weighted brain MRI volumes from clinical and population-based cohorts. We evaluate GSMR3D against geometry-specific specialists for isotropic, in-plane, and through-plane super-resolution using reconstruction quality and downstream tasks: brain tumour segmentation, brain-metastasis detection, and brain parcellation. GSMR3D outperforms specialists at isotropic $2\times$ and achieves better LPIPS, DISTS, and FID across all geometries, although specialists retain higher PSNR in anisotropic settings. It more faithfully preserves glioma segmentations across all geometries, achieves the highest lesion sensitivity on an independent brain-metastasis cohort, and the highest brain-parcellation agreement for isotropic and in-plane reconstruction. Reconstruction takes about 6s per volume, adding little overhead relative to acquisition-time savings. GSMR3D offers a flexible approach to MRI scan-time reduction with one model per contrast across geometries while preserving information relevant to downstream tasks.

Abstract: Video generation models synthesize visual sequences from text and image inputs. Extending video generation to long animations requires actions, dialogue, camera coverage, and narrative developments to remain coordinated across scenes. Executable scene programs make these decisions available for inspection. Constructing an entire story before observing its execution can leave later scenes dependent on unrealized events. We present SpriteStage, a progressive scene programming framework that builds animation through iterative enactment and engine reorchestration. Iterative enactment generates each scene from an accepted world checkpoint and narrative snapshot, executes it on a shared spatial stage, and uses runtime and visual feedback to revise the relevant actions or story beats. The accepted outcome becomes the starting state for the next scene. Engine reorchestration maps the resulting draft to production assets and engine capabilities while retaining its events, geometry, and timing. Inspection of the assembled sequence and frozen replay carry the accepted performance into the final video. Three complete animations, ranging from approximately 9.9 to 14.3 minutes, demonstrate the framework's effectiveness in producing extended narratives through scene programming and engine execution.

</details>

Abstract: Goal-conditioned manipulation policies are steered by a goal image, a picture of the desired post-manipulation scene synthesized from the current observation and a language instruction. The prevailing assumption is that better image-editing quality yields better goals, so goal generators are built and compared using general image-editing metrics. We show this assumption is false. Across two independent goal-conditioned policies on CALVIN, the image editors that top aggregate editing-quality leaderboards (Gemini 3 Pro Image and GPT Image 1.5) produce worse control targets than our proposed far smaller finetuned open models, and the gap widens sharply for the multi-step (long-horizon) tasks robots actually face. A per-dimension analysis explains why. Downstream policy success is governed almost entirely by manipulation adherence (selecting the right object and the right final state; Spearman $ρ\approx 0.9$ with success), while photorealism and aesthetic fidelity, precisely where large proprietary editors excel, are uncorrelated with success ($ρ\approx 0$). This dissociation holds on both CALVIN and the real-world-aligned SIMPLER-Bridge benchmark. We then present SGE-Goal, a spatially-grounded decomposed editing framework that predicts initial and final 2D edit-region masks before goal-image synthesis. SGE-Goal turns open base models (FLUX.1 Kontext, FLUX.2 klein) into the strongest goal generators we evaluate on CALVIN, and second only to Gemini 3 Pro Image on SIMPLER-Bridge. Base models fine-tuned with SGE-Goal also hold up under chaining far better than the best proprietary editor, sustaining roughly seven times the success rate at the longest (five-step) chains. We release code, datasets, checkpoints, and a human-aligned diagnostic evaluator. Our results argue that goal generators must be selected by downstream utility, not by editing-quality leaderboards.

</details>

Abstract: Machine vision-guided precision spraying is promising for sustainable vegetable production by enabling site-specific weed targeting, reducing herbicide application while protecting crop yield and quality. Field trials were conducted at 0.13, 0.27, and 0.45 m/s (i.e., 0.3, 0.6, and 1.0 mph) to characterize speed-dependent changes in weed detection, targeting, actuation, and crop damage. At the lowest speed of 0.13 m/s, the system achieved 94.6% mAP@50 and 93.2% recall for weed and crop detection. However, increasing platform speed substantially reduced system performance, with weed hit rate declining from 78.3% to 35.0% and crop contact rates increasing from 20.1% to 28.7%. Consequently, the system did not meet predefined commercial-viability thresholds. These results demonstrate that practical deployment requires improvements beyond weed detection alone, particularly coordinated improvements in motion-robust plant perception, spray-timing compensation, and crop-adjacent actuation precision. The identified performance bottlenecks provide specific engineering targets for advancing vision-guided precision spraying toward practical, high-speed vegetable weed management. The software programs developed in this study have been made publicly available to support future precision weeding research.

Abstract: Lipreading ability varies widely, including among people who are hard of hearing. Although mouth-directed attention is linked to speechreading outcomes, it remains unclear how much lipreading skill can be inferred from viewing behavior and which signals carry this information. We examine whether individual differences in lipreading ability are reflected in viewing behavior during silent speech and quantify the contributions of gaze dynamics, pupil-linked signals, and spatial viewing allocation. We introduce the Lipreading Perception Study (LiPS) dataset and a tracker-independent preprocessing pipeline that synchronizes SR Research EyeLink gaze and pupil recordings with the stimuli. Twenty-four adults (age 32 $\pm$ 10 years) completed a four-alternative forced-choice silent lipreading task. An extreme-groups protocol yielded 18 participants labeled as stronger or weaker lipreaders. Each participant-trial included four aligned views: lip-region images, frame-level gaze, pupil summaries, and spatial viewing proportions. A four-branch neural network with cross-modal attention predicted labels using participant-disjoint splits and systematic ablations. The full model reached accuracy 0.680 $\pm$ 0.040 across five folds, outperforming naive fusion baselines in accuracy. The pupil and viewing-summary branch was most informative: removing it caused the largest degradation (0.618 accuracy, 0.623 AUC), while it alone achieved 0.675 accuracy. The lip-image stream contributed little to participant-level discrimination. Individual differences in lipreading are reflected in pupil-linked and frame-level gaze signals rather than in how much time a viewer spends looking at the mouth. Synchronized gaze and pupillary measures may provide objective markers of visual speech perception, warranting investigation in larger, clinically diverse populations. LiPS provides a resource for extending this work.

</details>

Abstract: We present Stable Signer V3, an interactive high-resolution sign language avatar system built on 4D Deformable Primitive Splatting. It addresses three limitations of existing avatars: restricted facial expression, inaccurate identity-specific body deformation, and loss of fine appearance details. Shared Face Primitive Splatting adds transferable superquadric primitives for richer expression control; Test-Time Body Primitive Splatting learns an identity-specific virtual muscle layer for more faithful motion; and Test-Time Tunneling Super-Resolution uses TunnelingNet to recover identity-specific high-resolution details. Both test-time modules are optimized once per signer identity; inference then runs entirely from the stored identity parameters. Together, they reduce facial geometry error by 71.4%, body-boundary error by 35.1% across ten identities, and high-resolution RGB reconstruction error by 24.2% on held-out evaluations, while retaining interactive streaming.

Abstract: Recent advances in video generative models have enabled flexible conditional generation and editing pipelines, such as text-to-video, image-to-video, and reference-guided synthesis. However, these workflows are inherently one-directional, mapping conditions to videos without providing a mechanism to invert this process. In complex media production settings, the ability to recover controllable textual conditions from a given video is highly desirable, yet remains underexplored. While inverse prompting has been studied for image generation, directly extending these approaches to video is challenging due to the need to capture temporal dynamics beyond static appearance, as well as the substantial computational cost of per-video optimization. In this work, we propose the first practical framework for efficient video inverse prompting. Specifically, we formulate inverse prompting as a meta-learning problem and introduce Meta Video Inverse Prompting (MVIP), which learns a meta-prompt optimized over temporal dimensions to enable fast and effective prompt inference. Our approach amortizes the inversion process, eliminating the need for costly iterative optimization at test time. Experimental results demonstrate that MVIP recovers higher-quality inverse prompts with significantly improved reconstruction fidelity compared to image-based inversion and iterative captioning baselines, while achieving substantial gains in efficiency. Code and video results are available at https://ueriniuno.github.io/mvip-page/.

Abstract: Different visual token pruning strategies retain different subsets while maintaining similar task performance. This motivates a combinatorial question: how many visual subsets can preserve model behavior, and how does their abundance depend on the query? We introduce visual sufficiency landscapes to characterize this abundance. At a fixed layer and budget, visual sufficiency probability is the fraction of uniformly sampled subsets that jointly preserve the dense model's answer, reference-answer likelihood, and output distribution. Independent sampling replicates separate input variation from estimation noise. Across Qwen2.5-VL-7B, InternVL2-8B, and LLaVA-v1.5-7B, differences between inputs on GQA and TextVQA exceed replication error, and different questions about the same GQA image produce distinct landscapes. Sufficiency probability also improves failure prediction for full pruning methods. On LLaVA, adding this probability increases AUC by 0.1151 for FastV, 0.0683 for VisionZip, and 0.1401 for SparseVLM, while reducing log loss and Brier score. Several architecture-adapted configurations on the other two models reproduce these gains. Under controlled selection rules, predictors trained on the original images reduce log loss on new images by 9.0%, 13.0%, and 14.1%, respectively. These results establish successful-subset abundance as a measurable dimension of local prunability and connect it to the identification of high-risk pruning conditions.

This study proposes MultiAltitudeSR, a framework that conditions feature modulation and reconstruction on source and target ground sampling distances (GSDs). Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) encode images into dense visual token sequences, creating substantial attention and KV-cache overhead. Existing visual token compression methods reduce this cost by pruning low-saliency tokens in shallow layers, but this practice couples temporary computational deactivation with irreversible information deletion. We show that this coupling breaks an intrinsic inference pattern in MLLMs. Through layer-wise attention analysis, we identify \textbf{Visual Backtracking}: visual pivots remain spatially stable, yet in middle layers they actively retrieve context from tokens that appeared unimportant earlier. Thus, early low-saliency tokens can be inactive for computation while still being useful for later reasoning. Motivated by this finding, we propose \textbf{ReTrace}, a backtracking-aware compression framework that introduces a dormant token state. ReTrace caches non-pivot tokens outside expensive layer-wise computation, restores them during the backtracking window, and prunes only after their information has been absorbed. Experiments on Qwen2.5-VL and InternVL3 demonstrate nearly 90\% reductions in FLOPs and KV cache, a 2.83$\times$ prefilling speedup, and strong performance retention across complex vision-language benchmarks.

</details>

Abstract: Feed-forward 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) promises instant scene reconstruction with high-quality novel-view synthesis. Yet, fine-grained geometry and appearance remain difficult to recover, motivating us to revisit how feed-forward models represent, aggregate, and refine detailed visual information. We introduce FineSplat, a feed-forward 3DGS framework built around fine-grained image patches. Since global multi-view attention becomes prohibitively expensive at such high token resolutions, we develop a dual-grouping strategy that combines two complementary forms of locality: view-wise grouping enables efficient interaction among neighboring views, while 3D spatial grouping reconnects geometrically related tokens that may never have interacted under view-wise grouping by lifting them into the 3D scene space. We further leverage reconstruction error as direct feedback, providing an explicit rendering-based signal to recover fine-grained structures and appearance, thereby overcoming the limitations of one-pass feed-forward prediction. Finally, a compact Gaussian generation scheme preserves the benefits of fine-grained features without unnecessarily increasing the number of primitives. Together, these designs enable FineSplat to recover richer geometry and appearance while remaining efficient and compact, achieving state-of-the-art results by a substantial margin, under the same or smaller Gaussian budgets than existing methods.

Abstract: Affective image editing (AIE) aims to evoke a target emotion while preserving the content and structure of a source image. Existing methods improve emotional expression or visual fidelity, but the target emotion alone does not reveal which source regions should be changed and which should remain intact, especially in complex scenes. We introduce Interpretable Emotion Contribution Learning for Affective Image Editing (IECLA), a framework that separates source-emotion attribution from target-aware editing. IECLA constructs signed object-level supervision through counterfactual interventions and trains a context-conditioned contribution predictor to localize evidence that supports or opposes the source emotion. At inference, an image-level evaluator estimates the source emotion directly from the input, while the predictor identifies editable and preservation evidence without repeated interventions. A target-aware editing agent then translates this evidence and the desired emotion into localized, content-aware operations for existing editors. Extensive evaluations show that IECLA predicts counterfactual contributions more faithfully on both in-domain and cross-dataset tests, while external VLM and independent human references support the quality of its localized evidence. Across six editing backbones, IECLA consistently strengthens target-emotion expression while preserving image content and structure, and user studies further show improved perceived target expression and naturalness.

Abstract: We present Mixture-of-Wavelet-Experts (MoWE), a spatially adaptive routing mechanism that replaces max-pooling and strided convolutions in U-Net architectures for ultrasound lesion segmentation. Standard pooling operations suppress high-frequency spatial details, including potential boundary cues in speckled regions. Recent frequency-domain methods preserve some of these details, but a single wavelet family applies the same spectral bias across the image. MoWE instead uses a context-aware router to assign local regions to fixed wavelet families according to local texture and global feature context. The decoder reuses the encoder's spatial routing maps during the inverse transform. Learnable channel-wise gates rescale the high-frequency subbands before reconstruction. We evaluate MoWE with U-Net on four ultrasound lesion benchmarks (BUSI, TN3K, UDIAT, BUS-UCLM) and one cardiac dataset (CAMUS), and with nnU-Net on BUSI. On BUSI, MoWE improves U-Net Dice from 72.59\% to 74.55\% and reduces HD95 from 47.01 to 43.49\,px. With nnU-Net, MoWE improves Dice from 80.18\% to 81.97\% and reduces ASSD from 20.07 to 17.25\,px. On U-Net, MoWE reduces trainable parameters by 6.4\%.

Our results show that the proposed algorithm identifies highly compact and effective frame subsets for explaining model decisions. We also find that models capable of relying on a greater number of sufficient frame subsets tend to perform better, possibly because they are more robust, consistent with prior research. Abstract: Video Reasoning Segmentation (VRS) requires a model to identify an implicitly described target and predict its pixel-level mask trajectory, while resolving the spatial relations and temporal events that determine the answer. We introduce \textbf{Temporal Chain of Video Primitives (TCoVP)}, a spatiotemporally grounded form of Video CoT that organizes query-relevant observations into a chronological reasoning chain. Each primitive associates an entity reference with temporal support and timestamped spatial evidence, and the chain interleaves these references with language to guide reasoning about which entity is relevant, when the queried event holds, and where its evidence appears. The selected target primitives then provide box prompts and temporal support for video mask prediction. We construct TCoVP-SFT-15k and TCoVP-RL-13k to learn this representation through supervised fine-tuning and chain-aligned reinforcement learning. Extensive experiments across five VRS benchmarks and three model backbones demonstrate the strong performance of TCoVP, highlighting the effectiveness of organizing spatial and temporal evidence within the reasoning process. Abstract: Recognizing a vessel's type from an image supports maritime surveillance and sanctions monitoring, but a classifier can reach high accuracy by leaning on the generic hull instead of the more distinctive superstructure, and is then fooled when a vessel is re-hulled or occluded. De-biasing methods claim to remove such reliance, yet the field certifies them with worst-group accuracy, a proxy that needs group labels and only infers reliance from accuracy. We measure the reliance directly. HullProbe swaps a registered anatomical part of a vessel for another instance's and reads how much that part drives the decision, using a same-class swap to control for compositing artifacts. Because a surface vessel is a hull and a superstructure arranged along one axis, its parts form a 2x3 grid whose 2^6 = 64 swaps we evaluate exhaustively, and a single Walsh transform returns each part's interventional influence and its interaction spectrum. On planted shortcuts, where the true reliance is known, the measure tracks worst-group accuracy (r = -0.94 over 45 configurations), and replacing its learned segmenter with human annotations changes the hull-share by less than the audit's resolution. On the 26 vessel types of MARVEL, the classifier places about 46% of its corrected sensitivity on the generic hull yet follows the superstructure when the two conflict, and the group-label-free de-biasers available there do not detectably reduce the hull's share. The findings hold across three architectures and a second maritime dataset, and the method extends to fine-grained vehicle recognition on Stanford Cars.

</details>

Abstract: Fine-grained rectification of existing generations under a limited additional budget remains a key problem in test-time optimization (TTO) for image generation. As for prior approaches, they could identify residual errors, but could not directly ensure that the model acts on the feedback during reasoning. Such errors stem from multiple sources, such as the initial noise or text prompt, and ultimately induce an execution gap of failing to handle potential deviations along the trajectory generating. However, classic models generally cannot remedy this gap. To narrow it, we propose Reflection-Guided Dynamics Rectification (RGDR), which turns reflections on completed outputs into corrective interventions on the process of sampling dynamics at the trajectory level. Specifically, RGDR could impact every sampling step by adapting to instantaneous intervention. Moreover, RGDR generates flexible candidates while adjudicating between them and the reference. As experiments show, rectification from RGDR starts from the existing base trajectory and reflection, and RGDR is substantially more effective than former trajectory exploration. Under matched budgets for additional image generation, RGDR's gain in the cross-model average final checklist score over the reference is $1.51\times$ that of TIR prompt refinement and $1.81\times$ that of Dynamic CFG.

Abstract: Image dehazing is traditionally addressed through handcrafted physical priors or task-specific deep learning models, often requiring carefully designed architectures or extensive model training. Recent advances in tabular foundation models (TFMs), however, offer an alternative learning paradigm in which continuous relationships can be inferred directly from contextual examples without task-specific model training. Motivated by this capability, this paper investigates whether single-image dehazing can be effectively reformulated as an in-context tabular regression problem and introduces DehazeTab, a framework that estimates atmospheric transmission from structured image-patch representations. Specifically, overlapping patches extracted from hazy images are transformed into tabular samples using handcrafted haze-related descriptors, pretrained visual embeddings, or their hybrid combinations, while corresponding transmission patches are summarized as scalar regression targets. A pretrained TFM then performs in-context regression using labeled reference patches to predict transmission values for unseen images without any further fine-tuning. These predictions are spatially aggregated into a transmission map, refined through guided filtering, and incorporated into the atmospheric scattering model to recover haze-free images. Experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate competitive restoration performance and reveal that physically motivated handcrafted features can outperform pretrained visual embeddings, while hybrid representations offer complementary benefits. Further analyses examine the effects of feature representations, patch selection, and contextual reference configurations on restoration quality. Beyond image dehazing, the findings highlight the potential of TFMs as an alternative paradigm for addressing low-level vision tasks through structured and in-context inference.

</details>

Abstract: The success of chain-of-thought (CoT) in complex reasoning can be attributed to a textual scaffolding effect: explicit intermediate results can be examined, reused, and composed to support subsequent reasoning, much as scaffolding supports a building under construction. However, mainstream multimodal LLMs lack an equivalent scaffold for visual distinctions that are difficult to express in concise verbal descriptions. We therefore ask: Can native visual representations become first-class citizens alongside language, providing a visual scaffold for reasoning? We define native multimodal reasoning as reasoning in which native visual representations and language jointly carry reusable intermediate states. As a concrete implementation, the visual scaffolding mechanism we proposed lets the MLLM autonomously initiate a retrieval action at any reasoning step. A lightweight retriever selects native visual features, which are then incorporated directly into the evolving CoT and remain available to subsequent steps. To evaluate this mechanism under controlled visual demands, we introduce NMR-Bench, comprising Burden Counting and Path Tracing. With 88M added parameters (approximately 2%) on Qwen3-VL-4B-Thinking, our model achieves 66.43% and 72.50% accuracy, respectively, surpassing the evaluated GPT-5.5 and Gemini 3.1 Pro baselines. Controlled interventions show that changing selected visual content affects task accuracy and can redirect subsequent visual selection even when intervening text is held fixed. These findings show that native visual states can function as intermediate reasoning material and point toward reusable scaffolds for additional modalities.
