Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) fail at some tasks that are simple for humans---but why? We hypothesize that many such failures stem from VLMs' difficulties translating between vision and language representations, a misalignment the Platonic Representation Hypothesis predicts should disappear with scale. This paper is composed of two parts: in the first, we show that existing vision and language representations are linearly unalignable. This misalignment is an inherent property of the data and hence expected to persist regardless of either scale or the alignment's complexity. In the second, we demonstrate how this misalignment both leads to a predictably induced generalization failure and correlates with existing failure modes, where more representationally aligned VLM backbones accordingly exhibit fewer failures. Overall, these results imply representations will not become aligned simply given sufficient scale, providing evidence against a strong version of the Platonic Representation Hypothesis.

</details>

Abstract: High-definition maps provide critical structural information for downstream autonomous driving tasks. Recent methods increasingly rely on external priors to improve prediction accuracy and robustness. Yet, existing prior representations expose a trade-off: dense rasters are easy to integrate but ignore certain map geometric properties, while sparse vectors preserve geometric structure but require dedicated encoders and are difficult to plug into existing architectures. Abstract: Transformers are widely assumed to exhibit token-decoding explainability. In particular, in vision transformer (ViT) classifiers, decoding individual patch tokens has become a common diagnostic tool due to its tendency to yield human-interpretable outputs. In light of the increasing popularity of this approach, an important question is whether token-decoding explainability is an inherent property of transformers that should be expected to hold across architectures, scales, and training objectives, or merely an artifact of some implicit bias. In this paper we systematically study token-decoding explainability across diverse ViT settings, spanning architectures, scales, and training objectives, using both controlled experiments and real-world models. We find that while some models indeed exhibit aligned and interpretable token decodings, others exhibit either inverted explainability maps or a complete lack of correlation with the image semantics. As we show, these tendencies toward different explainability behaviors are not intrinsic to the architecture and can vary arbitrarily with changes in the optimization landscape. We provide theoretical support for these observations by demonstrating a setting where a single fixed toy transformer architecture can provably implement the optimal classification rule in multiple ways, which differ in their patch-decoding explainability patterns. Our results show that token-decoding explainability is not an inherent property of trained transformers, but rather an outcome contingent on the problem setting and thus should not be a-priori assumed to hold. Code can be found in https://ronensc.github.io/Explainability-Illusion/ .

Abstract: Existing motion transfer methods excel at exact, rigid motion replication from reference videos to target scenes, whereas they often fail to account for the compatibility between the transferred motion and the target scene environment in accordance with physical laws. In this work, we propose MotionShift, a novel motion transfer framework that achieves high-fidelity motion transfer while ensuring physical compatibility with the target scene. In particular, we design a planner--evaluator iterative motion adaptation mechanism, in which a Large Language Model (LLM) acts as the planner to generate motion trajectories by leveraging its strong semantic reasoning capabilities, while a Vision-Language Model (VLM) serves as the evaluator to rectify the motion based on its superior visual perception and scene understanding. Through this planner--evaluator synergistic reasoning process, the motion is adapted to the target scene in a training-free manner. The adapted motion is then used as a generative condition for an image-to-video (I2V) model to render videos in the target scene. Extensive experiments demonstrate that MotionShift achieves high-fidelity and physically compatible motion transfer across diverse and complex scenes. Moreover, we show that MotionShift can be readily integrated into existing trajectory-conditioned I2V models, endowing them with realistic motion transfer capabilities.

Abstract: Human memories are organized in a way that connects events with their corresponding spatial contexts. An AI assistant should also be able to memorize and recall the nexus of events and spaces. Thus we introduce SENRI, a task that studies this space--event connection in long, continuous real-life egocentric video. It contains six task types in two directions: Event$\rightarrow$Space, which recovers spatial knowledge from event cues, and Space$\rightarrow$Event, which retrieves events from regions and trajectories. To evaluate this, we build SENRI-Bench, containing 500 manually annotated multiple-choice questions over 10 uninterrupted self-recorded videos with around 25.3 hours of content. Also, we evaluate three typical long video understanding paradigms: direct perception, evidence seeking, and persistent memory. The task remains challenging across all three paradigms, highlighting substantial room for improvement. Then we show two representative failure modes of existing memory methods and address these evidence gaps with SENRI-Agent, a controller that uses Target-Guided Visual Check (TGVC) to propose local visual check tasks for parallel verifiers and integrate the reports into a working memory to help with reasoning. Built upon existing memory systems, SENRI-Agent improves the performance of existing baselines without modifying their stored memories. We hope SENRI will provide valuable insights into how real-life egocentric understanding should be evaluated, reveal the gaps in existing approaches, and offer a possible direction for improvement.

Abstract: Pathologic complete response (pCR) after neoadjuvant chemotherapy is a key prognostic indicator in aggressive breast cancer, yet existing prediction approaches either rely on pretreatment imaging alone, missing the patient's evolving response, or optimize multi-visit classifiers under direct outcome supervision, coupling representation learning with the clinical endpoint. A Factorized Response State pathway further decouples treatment-response dynamics from imaging appearance by encoding the structural prior that response manifests primarily in lesion morphology. After pretraining, all parameters are frozen and a linear readout predicts pCR at each clinical decision point. We evaluate CoRe-WM on I-SPY2 under five-fold cross-validation and assess cross-cohort transfer on I-SPY1. CoRe-WM achieves the highest discrimination among all evaluated methods at every prospective decision point, and demonstrates robust cross-cohort transfer. Ablation studies identify patient-profile conditioning and the factorized response pathway as the principal contributors. These results establish world-model-based representation learning as a promising framework for outcome prediction in oncology and beyond.

Abstract: Driving-scene reconstruction via feed-forward Gaussian splatting from sparse multi-view images remains challenging due to two factors. First, cross-camera photometric inconsistency, caused by variations in exposure, ISP, and illumination, can be incorrectly absorbed into Gaussian geometry. Second, unreliable local geometric supervision arises from normal-based constraints near depth discontinuities. To improve local geometric reliability, we estimate Fisher-information-based uncertainty from photometric sensitivity and use it to adaptively weight surface-oriented self-supervision. Geometry-oriented evaluations further reveal enhanced cross-view depth consistency and local surface-normal agreement, while qualitative results show fewer geometric artifacts and more coherent structures across overlapping views.

Abstract: Visual localization is critical for embodied agents, but deploying state-of-the-art deep feature matchers on edge devices is fundamentally limited by their massive computational cost. Standard deep matchers tightly interleave intra-image context building and inter-image matching at every layer, creating a severe interaction bottleneck. Localizing a single query against K database images forces the system to execute the full network K independent times with no capacity for caching. To break this bottleneck, we advocate for a paradigm shift toward cacheable image matching. We realize this paradigm with LateMatch, a versatile approach that restructures existing matchers by deferring inter-image interaction to their final layers. By isolating intra-image processing into offline-cacheable Context Blocks, only a small set of Interaction Blocks must be executed online. To further optimize LateMatch, we introduce E-SToRE (Entropy-guided Spatial Token Reduction and Expansion). E-SToRE leverages the decreasing Shannon entropy of the late Interaction Blocks to dynamically reduce token counts just prior to cross-image interaction. Evaluated across HPatches, ScanNet, MegaDepth, ETH-3D, and Aachen, LateMatch reduces effective computational cost by over 2x compared to standard deep matchers, while E-SToRE yields an additional 10% to 14% savings with negligible degradation in accuracy.

</details>

Abstract: Digital art generation has undergone a massive paradigm shift, moving from static image synthesis to dynamic video generation that can mimic the human painting process. While existing models can synthesize visually stunning time-lapses of paintings being created or "unpainted," they largely treat the creative process as a black box. Current conditioning mechanisms are either too rigid, relying on static geometric cues like Canny edges or depth maps, or too holistic, utilizing simple scalar progress variables that offer no localized control. Consequently, users lack the agency to steer how a painting evolves over time. In this work, we argue that true control over the video generation of artwork requires a joint understanding of two fundamental dimensions: where an artist acts, and when those actions take place. To achieve this, we introduce the Painting Change Map, a novel spatio-temporal control mechanism that explicitly tracks normalized structural changes across a 1,000-timestep continuum, a resolution sufficient to capture fine-grained stroke-level changes while remaining computationally tractable. By mapping localized visual differences into an encoded RGB space, our representation enables fine-grained guidance for both the forward painting and reverse unpainting processes. Extensive benchmarking demonstrates the efficacy of our approach. We outperform existing control baselines and achieve competitive or superior results compared to state-of-the-art painting methods. Furthermore, under oracle conditions, our method surpasses all baselines by a significant margin. Code is available at https://github.com/Markus-Pobitzer/painting-change-map.

Abstract: Image harmonization methods adjust the colour and lighting of an object pasted into a photograph so that it looks native to its new scene, and they are ranked by average error on one benchmark whose test images are real photographs with one object recoloured. One photograph can yield several such test images, and the benchmark promises that no photograph appears in both the training and the test split. We find that 1,399 photographs do, and the composites built on them are a quarter of the test set. Duplicate checks over file names or bytes miss this, because every composite is a different file. On that quarter the three methods we score end to end read two to five times lower error than on the rest, but most of the gap is composition rather than the sharing: the shared composites come almost entirely from one of the benchmark's four source collections, and inside that collection the gap falls to about one and a half times. An official score is therefore an average over two populations, mixed in a proportion the benchmark does not report. We release a test list of 5,581 composites whose construction identities do not appear in training, with four confirmed near-duplicate pairs disclosed rather than removed, adoptable without retraining at the price of one rescore per checkpoint. We leave open whether the sharing itself changes what a model learns.

Abstract: Visual autoregressive models produce convincing images but can misassign attributes and spatial relations. Within-prompt diagnostics identify attention-derived scores that predict final compositional preferences on Infinity, with category-dependent transfer to Switti. ATLAS uses these on-the-fly signals to progressively concentrate computation on promising trajectories. It maintains multiple coarse alternatives, prunes candidates without decoding at intermediate scales, and reserves image-space evaluation for a final choice among partial trajectories. ATLAS scales effectively on both backbones, achieving higher mean scores on the evaluated T2I-CompBench and GenEval categories as more computation is devoted to search. At the largest tested budget, the targeted T2I-CompBench mean improves by over 10% relative to each backbone. Separate profiles show over 50% less generation time than completing and evaluating all candidates at equal initial counts, alongside lower peak allocated GPU memory. Relative time savings are larger at the higher tested candidate count. Together, these results support scalable compositional search: additional sampling expands exploration, while attention-guided selection limits continuation cost without parameter updates or gradient-based optimization.

Abstract: Multi-turn image editing aims to modify an image according to a sequence of instructions, enabling more effective interactive visual content creation for users. However, existing methods often exhibit noticeable drift after only a few editing turns, manifesting in two aspects: 1) unstable visual quality, with accumulating artifacts and degraded details; and 2) inconsistent image content, with unintended changes to preserved regions and prior edits. To address these challenges, we propose MT Forcing, a novel training paradigm for multi-turn image editing with unified multimodal models (UMMs) based on autoregressive diffusion. Specifically, we propose Single-Turn Distribution Matching Distillation (Single-Turn DMD), which supervises student self-rollouts with a teacher that applies cumulative edits to the source image in a single turn, providing visual-quality guidance without recursive error accumulation. Additionally, we introduce Teacher-Forcing Guidance, which supervises history-dependent transitions on reference trajectories during warmup and as a regularizer during distillation, encouraging cross-turn content consistency beyond what per-turn distribution matching alone can ensure. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our approach significantly improves the multi-turn image editing capabilities of UMMs, yielding more stable visual quality and stronger cross-turn consistency.

Abstract: World models offer increasingly rich environments for exploration, yet intervening in a world while observing the consequences from freely chosen viewpoints remains challenging. We tackle these challenges by introducing UniState, a framework for controllable world simulation built on a unified world-state representation. This representation unifies spatial memory and user interventions. Users specify object motion directly within spatial memory, while independently choosing the camera trajectory from which the resulting world state is observed. This unification makes the relationship between the scene and the requested changes explicit, removing ambiguity between object motion and viewpoint change from the control specification. Crucially, specifying an intervention does not require prescribing every resulting movement: the video model can synthesize additional motion within controlled objects and plausible interactions with their surroundings. We develop training and sampling strategies that help generated videos follow the specified object motion and camera trajectory while preserving this capacity to generate responses beyond the prescribed transformations. Experiments demonstrate more accurate joint object and camera control than the existing methods, alongside qualitative evidence that intervention-induced scene changes persist across rollouts.

</details>

Abstract: Cross-modal alignment of synthetic aperture radar (SAR) imagery to natural language is constrained by two distinct failures. The first is modality mismatch: text encoders pre-trained on optical imagery, such as CLIP, cannot be efficiently fine-tuned on SAR, because active microwave backscatter is physically incompatible with passive optical reflectance and the available SAR caption datasets are orders of magnitude too small to bridge this gap without substantially degrading the learned semantic manifold. The second is physical forgetting: SAR encoders pre-trained on large-scale self-supervised objectives encode radar physics that are progressively erased when the encoder is fine-tuned for text alignment on limited caption data. A single design principle addresses both failures: keep both encoders frozen and learn only a constrained, structure-preserving bridge. Our Frozen Expert framework aligns a frozen CROMA SAR encoder with a frozen CLIP text encoder via a lightweight constrained residual bridge. We formally define physical forgetting via Procrustes disparity and show our bridge preserves SAR geometry significantly better than fine-tuning (disparity: 0.210 vs. 0.351). We validate generality across multiple SAR backbones and text encoders. On zero-shot SAR scene classification, our approach outperforms fine-tuned baselines using up to 8x less training data: DFC2020 (+10.3% R@1, +15.5% mAP), EuroSAT-SAR (+15.7% R@1, +12.2% mAP), BigEarthNet-MM (+14.7% R@1). These results establish frozen manifold alignment as a principled, data-efficient alternative to fine-tuning in high-disparity cross-modal settings.

</details>

Abstract: Photo Response Non-Uniformity (PRNU) is widely regarded as the most reliable marker for source camera identification. Recent studies, however, report high correlations between images and fingerprints from different modern smartphones, which calls into question the discriminative power of PRNU on these devices. Using 30 recent smartphones under controlled acquisition conditions (e.g., no digital zoom), we investigate whether the fingerprint itself is at fault or the way it is compared. We find that a component shared by all fingerprints of the same model inflates the standard signed peak-to-correlation energy (sPCE). To counter this, we propose $Δ$sPCE: the sPCE between a query image and a candidate camera's fingerprint, minus the mean sPCE between that image and the fingerprints of the remaining candidates, in the spirit of T-norm score normalization. $Δ$sPCE improves attribution accuracy, suggesting that the device-specific PRNU signal is still present. Although the limited dataset size and the dependence of $Δ$sPCE on the candidate camera set currently restrict operational applicability, these results suggest that PRNU-based attribution of modern smartphones remains feasible when fingerprints are compared appropriately.

Abstract: Multimodal perception improves autonomous-driving performance by combining complementary sensor information. However, stronger multimodal predictions do not necessarily imply stronger representations in each modality-specific branch. Such branch-level weaknesses can remain hidden in conventional output-level evaluation. We introduce UBD-Bench, a systematic benchmark for studying unimodal branch degradation (UBD) in multimodal perception. UBD-Bench uses a Paired Frozen-Branch Probe to compare the task-relevant representation quality of the same modality after independent unimodal training and multimodal joint training under matched decoding conditions. Across four autonomous-driving perception tasks and 10 models, we find that Camera branches consistently degrade after multimodal joint training, whereas LiDAR branches exhibit weaker and more model-dependent changes, revealing a pronounced modality asymmetry. Hierarchical probing further shows that Camera-branch degradation extends from the visual backbone through the Camera-to-BEV pathway and is most severe at the pre-fusion Camera BEV representation. Guided by this diagnosis, we introduce unimodal-to-multimodal distillation at the degraded representation. This intervention consistently improves both Camera-branch Probe performance and full-model performance across all four tasks. These results show that multimodal gains can coexist with degraded unimodal representations, highlighting branch-level representation quality as an important dimension for evaluating and improving multimodal perception.

In this work, we introduce noise-rejuvenated SMC, an inference-time scaling method tailored for text-to-image flow matching models. Our key idea is to perturb each resampled particle by changing its source noise and constructing a new intermediate state using its current endpoint estimate. This moves the particle to another state that remains representable under the flow-matching interpolation form, enabling particle exploration without converting the sampler to an SDE. We further introduce a look-ahead mechanism to refine resampled particles before weighting. Experiments on Stable Diffusion 3, FLUX.1-dev, and Z-Image demonstrate consistent improvements in reward-guided generation, while ablation studies validate the contributions of noise rejuvenation and look-ahead refinement. Abstract: To determine where computation is needed in volumetric segmentation networks, we analyze block-level redundancy and stage-wise token-mixer contribution. Centered Kernel Alignment reveals high input-output representational similarity within blocks of representative convolutional, hybrid, and Transformer-style segmentation networks. Similarity-guided pruning reduces their parameter counts by 39-75% while decreasing mean Dice by less than 0.4 percentage points. To examine token mixers within blocks, we introduce Anatomical Relation Distortion, which quantifies how bypassing a mixer distorts feature relations between labeled anatomical positions. Applied to an unpruned candidate architecture, Centered Kernel Alignment indicates high representational redundancy in intermediate encoder mixers, whereas Anatomical Relation Distortion shows that they contribute little and the bottleneck mixer most. These findings motivate SlimFormer++, which uses Selective Mixing blocks with windowed Mamba mixing in the first encoder stage and only residual channel MLPs in deeper stages. At the bottleneck, Augmented Mixing injects similarity-weighted sparse long-range context into the axial taps of a depthwise convolution at cost linear in voxel count. Evaluated against 12 baselines across eight public CT, MRI, and ultrasound datasets (1,809 cases), SlimFormer++ achieves the highest mean Dice (87.84%) and lowest mean HD95 (6.50 mm) with the fewest parameters (5.98 million). These results support stage-aware token mixing as a design strategy for improving the accuracy-efficiency trade-off in volumetric segmentation. Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) are asked to report what they see: to count objects, box them, parse a screen or pick a click target (GUI grounding). We argue that reporting is part of the visual task, not merely its formatting. In a dense scene with 173 shapes, GPT-6.1 lists 173 records that match every shape, yet the counts it states in the same response sum to 188. From Seeing to Reporting (FSR) is a theory of visual reporting and a set of inference-time procedures that improve the reports of frozen VLMs. It models reporting as a chain from responses to decoded records, constructed sets and task answers, linked by task-compatible matching. The chain gives exact relations for deleting records, averaging their coordinates and switching readouts, and it splits the error of one answer chosen among several calls into missing candidates, lost representatives and wrong choices. These relations guide procedures for counting, detection, document layout, screen parsing and GUI grounding that use the task specification and a shipped configuration, without requiring labels from the target task. On 50 dense synthetic scenes, a one-call reporting-and-decoding protocol cuts per-type count error by 85.2% for GPT-6.1 and 98.8% for Claude Sonnet 5.5. On 600 ScreenSpot-Pro items, a GUI-grounding tier that combines three models lowers the miss rate from 13.67% for GPT-6.1's two-stage zoom to 9.33%, against 12.67% for three such zooms combined by a cluster vote at about the same cost. A plain vote over the same three models misses 12.33%: the other models supply the candidates, and selecting among them turns those candidates into fewer misses. We release the protocols, the stored responses and the analysis code.

</details>

Abstract: We introduce ReDT, a feed-forward visual geometry transformer designed to scale multi-view reconstruction to high-definition (HD) inputs. Existing visual geometry transformers are typically trained at fixed, standard definition (SD) resolutions and exhibit degraded reconstruction quality and poor computational scaling to high input resolutions. While recent efficient architectures reduce the complexity of inter-frame attention, expensive intra-frame and encoder attention remain largely unaddressed, making HD reconstruction impractical with existing architectures. ReDT addresses both limitations with a resolution-aware architecture designed to scale to high-resolution inputs. The key idea of ReDT is to first map each view to a fixed-size set of basis tokens, compute correspondences in this compact constant-size basis space, and then project the outputs back to the original resolution for dense prediction. By decoupling the internal representation from input resolution during attention, ReDT substantially reduces the computational cost of high-resolution 3D reconstruction and improves model performance at all resolutions. Extensive experiments on multiple datasets spanning SD to FHD (2K) show that ReDT outperforms state-of-the-art visual geometry transformers in both reconstruction quality and computational efficiency. For instance, on the ETH3D (2K) dataset, ReDT achieves a $16\times$ speedup, a $34\%$ reduction in peak GPU memory usage, and a $33\%$ reduction in chamfer distance over VGGT-$Ω$. \href{https://redt.github.io}{Project Website.}

Abstract: Rapid advances in image synthesis have increased the need for reliable synthetic-image detectors, yet their robustness to transferable attacks remains incomplete and understudied. Existing evaluations generally examine whether an attack constructed against one detector transfers to another, but rarely isolate how its effectiveness changes when the attacked images originate from different generators. We conduct a systematic evaluation of three representative attacks: two iterative perturbation-based methods, MIG and MuMoDIG, and the amortized generative attack Tricker. Across four detectors and five generators spanning diverse architectures, we introduce a two-axis evaluation protocol that independently varies the surrogate detector used to construct or train the attack and the generator supplying its input images. We assess attack effectiveness, cross-detector transferability, perceptual quality, runtime, and interaction with common post-processing operations. We revisit Tricker and show that its data and training requirements can be substantially reduced, enabling broader and more efficient evaluation. Our results demonstrate that transferability is not a detector-only property: the input-generator distribution strongly influences both evasion effectiveness and attacked-image quality. MIG and MuMoDIG exhibit pronounced dependence on the detector--generator pair, whereas Tricker provides more uniform white-box evasion and image quality. We additionally observe that post-processing maintains the asymmetric transferability topology. These findings expose a neglected dimension of attack evaluation and motivate transfer-aware protocols that jointly account for detector and generator variation. We will release our code, evaluation data, and trained attack models with a responsible-use statement.

</details>

Abstract: A central challenge in multimodal learning is how to fuse modalities that differ substantially in predictive strength. To address this problem, we propose CheXFuse, a multimodal fusion framework based on Guided Differential Joint Attention (GuDJA). Abstract: Vision-based object pose estimation is vital for conducting spacecraft rendezvous and proximity operations (RPO). Recent research has demonstrated the robustness of event cameras against the harsh lighting conditions that often occur in orbit, thanks to the extremely high dynamic range of event sensors. However, event-based spacecraft pose estimation (EBSPE) suffers when the relative motion between the chaser and target is slow, since the event rate drops significantly, leading to data with low signal-to-noise ratio. Unfortunately, for safety reasons, real RPO trajectories often involve slow relative motions (e.g., down to 1 mm/s), and operating under low illumination exacerbates the problem. Addressing slow relative motions is thus crucial to raise the practicality of EBSPE. To this end, we propose using recurrent vision transformers (RVT) for EBSPE. The key idea is to exploit recurrent memory to capture longer-term information across sparse event streams, and to learn the temporal attention span from full trajectories rather than fixing the time window. We developed an RVT-based heatmap regressor to produce 2D-3D correspondences for pose estimation, which was validated on real event data collected under slow relative motions representative of RPO trajectories. Results indicate the superior pose estimation accuracy and stability of our RVT-based method, relative to previous per-event-frame-based or fixed-time-window EBSPE methods.

Abstract: Unsupervised cross-domain person re-identification remains challenging because domain shifts generate noisy pseudo labels that are reinforced during iterative adaptation. CGPLR estimates sample-level reliability by jointly modeling centroid compatibility, local neighborhood consistency, separation from the strongest competing cluster and temporal assignment stability. The resulting confidence distribution is used to derive cluster-adaptive upper and lower thresholds for a three-way selective decision. Reliable assignments are preserved, recoverable samples are reassigned only when an alternative cluster receives sufficient geometric and neighborhood support and highly ambiguous samples are deferred from hard identity supervision. Abstract: Autoregressive (AR) video diffusion models generate streaming video of arbitrary length by conditioning each new chunk of frames on those already generated. However, they suffer from exposure bias, where a model trained on clean context must continue from its own imperfect outputs, and errors compound until quality collapses within seconds (drift). Existing methods either require cluster-scale base retraining, apply training-free inference corrections with inconsistent results, or train frozen-base correctors for sampling and cache errors. We instead find that drift is largely deterministic and therefore learnable, as holding the noisy state fixed while varying only the history reveals a counterfactual velocity gap that is ~95% systematic across noise seeds. Based on this observation, we introduce Clean Forcing, which trains a 5.9M-parameter LoRA corrector on the frozen base model to regress this gap, with a closed-loop stage that adds DAgger-style aggregation and a drift-contraction objective to expose the corrector to its own rollouts. Because the clean histories can be the base model's own single-shot generations, training needs no real videos, and the merged corrector adds no inference cost. In 50 s text-to-video generation, Clean Forcing reduces Δ-drift from +11.3 to +1.65 without real video and to -0.16 with 40 real clips, improving on the best published result of +3.57 with over 150x less data. Extensive experiments demonstrate that Clean Forcing outperforms prior methods in aesthetic quality and data efficiency with reduced drift for long-horizon video generation.

</details>

Abstract: Medical image registration is a fundamental task for aligning anatomical structures across images. Exhaustively searching for point-wise correspondences is prohibitively expensive in both computation and memory, particularly for high-resolution 3D medical images. Most recent learning-based methods directly predict correspondences in a coarse-to-fine manner, but often propagate intermediate estimates across scales without explicitly weighting correspondence evidence, which can allow coarse-scale errors to persist during refinement. To address these challenges, we propose ACorD, an adaptive correspondence discovery framework designed to mitigate error propagation with an attention-guided process. ACorD first predicts multiple potential correspondence candidates through Correlation-guided Cross-scale Offset Prediction (CCOP), which combines correlation maps as spatial matching priors with cross-scale features as global deformation context. Then, Multi-Head Deformable Aggregation (MHDA) aggregates these candidate correspondences using attention, producing a learned, non-uniform weighting over competing matches rather than treating them equally. The multi-head design further captures complementary deformation patterns, ensuring both global alignment and local precision. By integrating adaptive offset prediction with selective, attention-based aggregation of correspondence evidence, ACorD enables accurate deformation refinement. Extensive experiments on four public medical registration benchmarks demonstrate that adaptive correspondence discovery within a multi-scale refinement framework improves registration robustness across diverse anatomical structures.

Abstract: Diffusion and flow matching models achieve impressive visual generation quality but remain computationally expensive due to iterative sampling. Caching-based acceleration provides a practical training-free solution by reusing or predicting intermediate features. However, existing methods largely enforce a forward-only information flow: approximations produced for skipped model evaluations are immediately committed to the sampling trajectory and cannot benefit from feedback generated by later full-computation steps. We propose \textbf{PoSTCache} (\emph{Postdictive Smoothing Trajectory Cache}), a training-free and plug-and-play framework that reformulates diffusion caching as revisable trajectory inference. PoSTCache first uses an existing caching strategy to construct forward proposals for skipped steps. At a later full-computation step, the discrepancy between the refreshed prediction and its forward proposal yields a correction signal, which PoSTCache propagates backward to refine and reintegrate the preceding predictions. Viewing full-computation steps as sources of corrective feedback also changes how they should be selected. We therefore introduce the Posterior Variance Reduction (PVR) strategy, which estimates cross-timestep dependencies from offline denoising trajectories and selects anchors according to their expected reduction in surrogate trajectory uncertainty rather than local approximation error alone. Experiments on representative image and video generation models show that PoSTCache consistently improves the quality--efficiency trade-off over representative caching methods.

Abstract: Streaming video world models generate the future chunk by chunk, conditioned on their own past and a stream of actions. Trained on short clips, they drift once a rollout exceeds the training horizon, as prediction errors re-enter the context and compound. A common remedy is to train the model on its own rollouts via distribution matching distillation (DMD). For action-conditioned world models, the teacher becomes a bottleneck: an external video model is agnostic to the actions, whereas the model's own pretrained network drifts in the same manner as the student. We observe that a long rollout need not match a teacher, provided it remains a realistic continuation of its own scene that follows the commanded actions. Realism and scene fidelity can be measured against real-data statistics, which remain valid at any horizon, while action following is preserved by the model's conditional generation loss. We propose Fréchet Forcing, which fine-tunes a world model together with its deployed multi-step sampler under three constraints. Generative anchoring applies the flow-matching loss after a self-generated history ending on real data. Context fidelity, a Fréchet distance (FD) on latent statistics of the free rollout centred on each clip's own ground truth, keeps every rollout faithful to its scene. Reality matching, an FD to real frames in a frozen perceptual space, keeps the generated frames realistic. Because all three targets are derived from short real clips without comparing a rollout against a long recorded future, training requires no teacher, no online critic and no long videos. Fine-tuning MiniWorld-1B, pretrained on 253-frame clips, for at most 36 GPU-hours on 381-frame clips yields rollouts that remain stable beyond 4,000 frames and continue to follow their actions in both robot manipulation and camera-controlled exploration.

</details>

Abstract: Reference-based 3D scene stylization transfers the appearance of a reference image onto a reconstructed 3D scene while preserving scene geometry. Recent methods perform this transfer by optimizing the appearance representation of neural scene models including Neural Radiance Fields (NeRF) and 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS). These methods optimize an RGB appearance representation, in which a single per-point colour combines the effects of material reflectance, environment illumination, together with the shading it produces, and the viewing direction. When stylization modifies this combined colour, the illumination effects present during stylization are baked into the stored appearance, so the stylized scene cannot be rendered under different illumination. To address this limitation, we present RxGS, a relightable 3D scene stylization framework built on a Bidirectional Reflectance Distribution Function (BRDF)-aware 3D Gaussian representation. Rather than modifying a per-point RGB colour, RxGS optimizes only the diffuse albedo field recovered by inverse rendering, while leaving roughness, metallicity, surface normals, and the estimated environment lighting unchanged. As a result, the stylized scene keeps all the components a physically-based renderer needs other than the edited albedo, and can therefore still be relit. RxGS optimizes the albedo field using a nearest-neighbour feature matching loss together with a spatial smoothness prior over Gaussian centres. We evaluate the method using a Geometry Consistency Cost (GCC) based on optical-flow warping error. RxGS achieves the lowest GCC on all evaluated NeRF-Synthetic scenes and remains competitive on real-captured scenes, while additionally supporting relightable stylization, which existing reference-based 3D scene stylization methods do not provide.

Abstract: Modern generative models can produce visually plausible images while violating exact identity constraints. Evaluating such failures requires distinguishing violations of the requested identity from permissible variation in appearance. We introduce Low-Entropy Visual Identity (LEVI), which characterizes visual identities determined by compact facts and explicit rules. The low-entropy requirement concerns identity uncertainty, while appearance may remain diverse. We formulate identity fidelity in terms of the probability that a conditional image distribution assigns to the identities permitted by the prompt. Visual Identity Spaces (VIS) formalize this distinction by specifying admissible identity values and which different realizations express the same identity. Building on this formulation, we develop Verifiable Visual Grammar (VVG), a declarative language whose typed primitives and compositional constraints describe identity-bearing structures. Using this grammar, we build a benchmark for image generation. It assesses each output against the task's identity requirements, drawing evidence from the final pixels. The curated benchmark contains 1,375 tasks spanning 120 base identity spaces across six domains. Among ten evaluated model configurations, the highest whole-task pass rate is 58.69%. Abstract: Autonomous driving fundamentally demands dense understanding of visual, semantic, and geometric information, yet existing Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models are typically trained with sparse supervision such as language instructions or trajectory signals. This direct mapping from dense visual observations to sparse representations forces the model to compress or even discard critical scene information essential for safe driving. We argue that this limitation does not stem from insufficient model capacity, but from the lack of explicit mechanisms that encourage the formation of dense world representations in the latent space. In this spirit, we propose a novel paradigm for driving intelligence, Think Densely, Act Sparsely, and instantiate it with an efficient and interpretable framework, LECDrive (Latent Expert Cognitive Chains for Vision-Language-Action autonomous Driving). The core idea is to mimic the hierarchical perception process of humans by embedding a progressive chain of latent expert cognition within the VLA model. Concretely, we inject a compact set of task-specific latent tokens as carriers, enabling the model to internalize dense knowledge from foundational vision models, including visual representations (DINOv3), semantic structures (SAM3), and spatial geometry (DepthAnything3), while maintaining efficient inference. This interdependent cognitive chain follows a curriculum learning principle, where supervision is progressively structured from representation to semantics and geometry. Extensive experiments demonstrate that LECDrive consistently improves the performance of base VLMs across multiple autonomous driving benchmarks, covering key tasks such as object perception, state prediction, and trajectory planning. These findings suggest that constructing dense world models through structured latent expert chains is a promising direction for VLA-based autonomous driving.

Abstract: Generative image super-resolution produces visually realistic high-resolution images, but may also introduce hallucinations by adding unsupported content or altering existing details. Because existing super-resolution metrics fail to measure whether generated details remain grounded in the reference image, we present a hallucination assessment technique based on bidirectional visual question answering (VQA), which we term VQA-based Hallucination Assessment (VHA). The proposed approach uses a vision-language model (VLM) to determine whether the semantic content and low-level visual evidence in a super-resolved image are supported by its ground-truth counterpart. To this end, it constructs question--answer pairs from each image and evaluates them on both images. Discrepancies between the resulting scores in both directions reveal missing or altered ground-truth information and unsupported additions. However, obtaining these scores requires computationally expensive VLM inference. We therefore develop VHA+, a learned, lightweight model that directly predicts VHA scores from ground-truth and super-resolved image pairs. We construct a hallucination benchmark, VHA-bench, through a human study and show that both VHA and VHA+ correlate highly with human judgments of hallucination. Abstract: Open-vocabulary human-object interaction detectors describe interactions in free text, and this creates a problem for evaluation. Category scoring rejects valid paraphrases, while semantic similarity scoring can reward predictions that name the wrong action or reverse the roles of the participants. Abstract: Game world models have made rapid progress toward real-time interactive generation. Yet action control is still predominantly implemented as feature conditioning, leaving the model to learn from training data how each action should move the visual world. This formulation is poorly matched to a broad class of planar-camera worlds, such as top-down and isometric games. In these worlds, player motion induces a geometrically well-defined planar camera displacement. We observe that the key to spatially consistent control in such worlds is to represent the effect of each action directly in world coordinates. To this end, we introduce ActionRoPE, a parameter-free action interface that converts action-driven camera displacement into world-coordinate rotary positions. As a result, tokens corresponding to the same world location retain the same positional address across time. These world coordinates further expose a spatial asymmetry in generation: regions covered by the initial observation are geometrically known, while newly revealed regions remain to be synthesized. We therefore propose Coordinate-Conditioned Noise Scheduling (CNS), which assigns token-wise denoising schedules according to this known-new structure. It allows observed regions to stabilize earlier, while new content is generated with reference to them. Compared with four representative action-conditioning mechanisms, our approach follows the commanded motion more faithfully and reduces the revisit drift by 66% over the strongest baseline. The same interface transfers across video backbones from 1.3B to 33B parameters and to out-of-distribution scenes. Project Page: https://teawhite.cn/ActionRoPE/

Abstract: Diffusion models primarily rely on general-purpose ODE-based numerical methods for training-free accelerated sampling, but performance degrades with coarse time discretization. Abstract: Video vision-language models (VLMs) have strong semantic understanding, yet remain unreliable at precise temporal grounding. Existing gains from explicitly aligning video frames with absolute time suggest that VLMs may already encode accurate temporal locations internally but lose precision when expressing them as timestamps. Our analysis across four VLMs and two grounding datasets confirms this hypothesis: attention intervals outperform predicted intervals on approximately 66% of valid examples. We term this discrepancy the temporal generation gap. Because precise temporal annotations are expensive, this finding motivates closing the gap to improve temporal grounding using only unlabeled video-query pairs. We propose Self-Supervised Attention Policy Optimization (SAPO), which uses internal temporal attention as self-supervision for Group Relative Policy Optimization and applies a ground-truth-free criterion to select high-value samples. Abstract: Joint 4D reconstruction of a human and object from a monocular video is essential for understanding how people interact with their environment. While recent methods have made noticeable progress, capturing such human-object interactions remains difficult due to two main challenges. First, a video typically contains composite semantic context spanning multiple interaction phases, making it difficult to track the temporal changes of the interaction in the reconstruction. Second, under human-object occlusion, a single-view observation contains incomplete visual information about the interaction, resulting in geometrically inconsistent reconstructions. To address these challenges, we introduce HORIA, built upon two core designs. First, we decompose the entire input video into temporally grounded and semantically distinct interaction phases with motion-aware captioning, obtaining a specific text description for each phase. Second, conditioned on these text descriptions of the interaction, we propose an interaction-aware re-rendering that compensates for the limited single-view observation. Specifically, we render the initially reconstructed 4D human and object from novel viewpoints and refine the renderings with a text-to-video diffusion prior, which provides auxiliary novel-view supervision beyond single-view cues. As a result, our framework produces temporally coherent and accurate 4D reconstructions under diverse interaction scenarios, achieving state-of-the-art performance.

Abstract: We set out to reproduce a recent hallucination-mitigation result on the POPE benchmark. The LLaVA-1.5-7B baseline we measured, 82.21 F1 on the adversarial split, sat 1.21 to 8.67 points above the adversarial baselines we planned to compare against (81.00, 79.30, and 73.54). We looked at the evaluation code and found a readout choice that moves F1 on its own. A two-token logit readout decides yes versus no by comparing two vocabulary IDs, 3582 ("yes") and 1217 ("no"). Across 9,000 greedy-decode questions spanning all three splits, neither ID is generated even once. The model emits " Yes" (token 3869) and " No" (token 1939), because SentencePiece prepends a space byte after the prompt suffix "ASSISTANT:". Read with an eight-token lookup that covers the space-prefixed surface forms, LLaVA-1.5-7B scores F1 0.8221, 0.8498, and 0.8713 on the adversarial, popular, and random splits. That 6.13-point adversarial shift exceeds the adversarial gain reported by five of the six papers we surveyed. None of them states its readout, and our reference ran under 4-bit NF4 quantization, so we cannot say how much of any reported gain a matched evaluation would keep. That comparison stays unresolved until readout and precision are matched. On the fixed baseline we run nine inference-time corrections of our own (none beat it) and dissect why the adversarial split resists logit-level intervention. In a 3,000-question probe, wrong predictions draw more image attention than correct ones (Cohen's $d=-0.46$, $p=1.4\times10^{-24}$), the opposite of what grounding-based corrections assume. A four-readout audit across four further VLMs from three tokenizer families confirms the confound is protocol-level, not model-specific: porting the LLaVA-1.5 IDs verbatim drops F1 to 0.00 on the two disjoint-vocabulary models, whereas a tokenizer-derived readout holds between 0.80 and 0.92.

Abstract: Thin cylindrical objects offer little space for planar markers. We propose a dual-colour-stripe marker that uses the known curvature of an object to improve pose measurement with a single camera. The boundary between two colours provides a long centre line, while dots and printed edges supply features at different depths. We analyse how this depth separation improves the estimation of rotation about the cylinder axis, or roll. For a camera-facing marker at 15 cm, the Cramér-Rao lower bound on roll standard deviation is 2.00°for a flat marker, 0.38°for a single curved marker and 0.21°for a wrap-around marker. Monte Carlo roll RMS errors are within about 10% of the corresponding standard-deviation bounds. A first-order relation connects the sagitta of the curved marker to the roll sensitivity provided by a stereo baseline. The analytical and simulation results explain how curvature improves monocular measurement and provide a basis for designing marker geometry.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, particularly Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), has improved video reasoning in multimodal large language models. However, standard video GRPO varies only linguistic trajectories while conditioning every rollout in a group on the same sampled frames, which can produce homogeneous rewards and weaken group-relative learning when the shared visual evidence is insufficient or distracting. We propose \textbf{FrameGRPO}, a simple and effective reinforcement-learning framework that requires neither auxiliary networks nor changes to the model architecture. Specifically, FrameGRPO combines two lightweight components: (1) \textbf{Multi-Frame Rollouts}, which use only uniform temporal subsampling of a single maximal-frame decode to construct aligned views that diversify visual evidence and reasoning trajectories; and (2) \textbf{Gated Temporal Efficiency Credit}, which selectively reinforces a correct lower-ratio trajectory when all rollouts in a fixed full-ratio reference subset fail, without imposing an unconditional preference for fewer frames. Extensive experiments across diverse video understanding and reasoning benchmarks demonstrate robust overall improvements over standard GRPO, highlighting temporal-view diversification as an effective mechanism for exploration and credit assignment in video MLLMs.

</details>

Abstract: Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) deliver high-fidelity image and video generation, but inference requires many sequential model evaluations. Two acceleration strategies are increasingly important: model compression makes each evaluation cheaper, while adaptive inference avoids selected teacher computation through reuse, forecasting, or speculative execution. Their combination, however, is brittle: directly applying the evaluated adaptive methods to a compressed student can degrade quality, an observed failure mode we call the \textbf{Combination Gap}. We link this gap to a mismatch between pointwise distillation and short-horizon execution: a student trained at teacher states need not remain reliable when its own predictions are chained. We propose \textbf{TamedFlow}, which trains a compressed DiT for this deployed role through cross-architecture feature alignment, a near-identity Velocity Alignment Head, and randomized rollout supervision. At inference, a lightweight Learnable Trajectory Estimator routes completed drafts: accepted drafts bypass the teacher, while rejected drafts roll back and receive one teacher rescue step. Distilling DiT-XL/2 into DiT-B/2, TamedFlow reaches FID $2.58$ at $2.25\times$ measured speedup over DDIM-50 on ImageNet $256\times256$. Ablations support trajectory supervision, and routing controls favor LTE over random decisions at nearly matched latency. Evaluations on ImageNet $512\times512$, MS-COCO, and Latte/FaceForensics extend the empirical scope.

Abstract: Once historical clients become unavailable, a federation must serve newcomers through the artifact it leaves behind. How much of its knowledge remains useful in an architecture chosen afterward? We introduce FEDNBO, a seven-dataset benchmark that separates knowledge supply, policy identification, and private-model compilation under disjoint data roles. Controlled studies expose inflated oracle gains, architecture-dependent transfer reversals, and a gap between teacher accuracy and private-model utility. Ordinary public-response distillation provides a competitive reference without public task labels or newcomer samples: gains over strengthened local training are 23.52 and 16.62 percentage points on 300 CelebA identities and 300 FedEMNIST writers, respectively. In a complete-pipeline FedEMNIST comparison, this reference reaches 97.13% accuracy with 12.4 seconds of compilation per architecture, versus 97.03% and 67.5 seconds for a HeteroFedTTA reconstruction at the same epoch budget. Equal-count public-input interventions extend support sensitivity to trainable visual backbones. Together, these results establish an auditable benchmark and a strong reference for evaluating the utility, cost and reliability of post-federation knowledge transfer.

Abstract: External skills provide language-model agents with reusable procedural knowledge, yet how models use such knowledge after it is supplied remains poorly understood. We identify Procedural Exposure Bias (PEB), the tendency of language models to increasingly favor an action as its associated procedural pattern is repeatedly exposed in a skill, even when the interaction state and action relevance remain unchanged. We find that PEB strongly depends on how procedural knowledge is represented: presenting the same content visually substantially attenuates exposure-induced shifts in action preference relative to textual presentation. Further analysis shows that this attenuation primarily arises from visual encoding rather than structured layout. Although repeated procedural signals remain recoverable from Visual representations, they exert a weaker influence on downstream action prediction, distinguishing signal accessibility from its use as action evidence. This effect also extends to executable action selection, where increasing exposure more strongly favors the corresponding action under Text than Visual. Layer-wise analyses show that exposure-dependent action evidence becomes more pronounced toward the model output, with stronger evidence under Text, while activation interventions reveal architecture-specific roles of internal components in transmitting or counteracting the preference shift. Complete-agent evaluations across ALFWorld, ScienceWorld, and WebShop further show that Visual skills achieve higher macro-average task success than Text skills for all four evaluated models. These findings establish skill representation as an important factor governing how procedural knowledge is translated into agent decisions. All code is available at https://github.com/NEUIR/Vis-Skill.

Abstract: Automated treatment planning (ATP) aims to reduce the unwarranted inter-planner variability of manual planning, while improving treatment quality and efficiency. Most ATP pipelines rely on the prediction of the achievable three-dimensional dose distribution for a given anatomy and the subsequent optimization of machine parameters to reproduce it. Diffusion-based methods are largely slice-wise due to the high dimensionality of radiotherapy data, and assume a one-to-one mapping between anatomy and optimal dose despite the multi-objective nature of treatment planning. They are evaluated on their ability to match reference doses and rarely on their integration in ATP frameworks. We present LaDose, a latent diffusion framework for fully volumetric dose prediction. An autoencoder learned on dose distributions, trained so that perturbed latents decode to plausible doses, compresses dose maps 128-fold and enables 3D diffusion on a single GPU. Prescription values are represented as exact scalar vectors that bypass the image encoder, avoiding resampling and latent compression. Multiscale classifier-free guidance allows for the modulation of clinical trade-offs at inference, without retraining or additional labeled data. On 900 clinically delivered head-and-neck volumetric modulated arc therapy plans from five centers, LaDose reached a median absolute error of 1.18 Gy (1.4--1.8\% of the prescription) and was the only one of six frameworks free of systematic bias with respect to the reference distribution. Guidance scales acted predictably on target coverage and organ-at-risk sparing. Integrated with direct aperture optimization, the pipeline generated plans in 15.2 minutes; in a blinded review, senior clinicians rated 14 of 15 plans acceptable without editing.

Abstract: Despite achieving high visual fidelity, video models still suffer from physical hallucinations, limiting their reliability as world simulators. Existing methods leverage model-internal or external signals to improve physical plausibility, but their roles and limitations require a unified explanation. We propose a two-dimensional physical-hallucination risk framework that defines low-density sampling risk and high-density drifting risk. The first risk captures generations assigned low likelihood by the video model, for example, a ball suddenly teleporting despite the model favoring continuous motion, while the second risk captures physical inconsistencies in high-likelihood generations, for example, a ball bouncing progressively higher without additional energy. Our analysis explains how model-internal signals steer sampling toward higher-density regions of the learned distribution to address low-density sampling risk. However, higher model likelihood does not guarantee physical plausibility, motivating external guidance to address high-density drifting risk. Based on this framework, we develop Hallucination-Aware Sampler (HA-Sampler), which applies external physical guidance within latent refinement to generate physically plausible videos, and HA-Scorer, which derives a likelihood-based signal from the generator itself for evaluating generated videos using continuous normalizing flow (CNF), with coarse ODE integration and finite-difference divergence estimates. Experiments on Physics-IQ and VideoPhy benchmarks support our theoretical analysis and demonstrate that our methods outperform the compared sampling and scoring SOTA baselines.

Abstract: Synthetic data provides the means for more ethical and less biased facial recognition. Nonetheless, the limitations of generative models and the conditioning process they rely on pose a risk of identity leakage, resulting in synthetic samples that are overly similar to those seen in a generator's training set, mitigating privacy benefits. In this work, we show that previously proposed approaches are affected by two key limitations: high classifier dependence and a tendency to identify leakage with respect to real datasets that were not used in training. To address these shortcomings, we propose a novel metric called Scaled Overlap (SO), which adapts a non-parametric data-copying test for facial recognition and extends it to the identity level. By leveraging a multi-model setup that compensates for errors in individual classifiers, it provides insights into the true strength of identity leakage. Our analysis identifies leaking datasets (CemiFace, SFace2, IDNet) with the most acute leakage introduced by generative model conditioning in CemiFace, demonstrates that real datasets do not contribute equally to generator training, and shows that the majority of synthetic images are drawn from multiple identities rather than relying on a single identity. Ultimately, our study provides new insights into the quality of synthetic datasets and gives more reliable techniques to help the community ensure the trustworthiness of synthetic data.

Abstract: Zero-shot composed image retrieval (ZS-CIR) aims to retrieve a target image that reflects an intended textual modification while maintaining semantic consistency with a reference image. Existing training-free ZS-CIR methods typically rely on fine-grained generated target descriptions, which often overemphasize reference-specific visual details and lead to appearance-biased retrieval. Moreover, since these descriptions are generated through multimodal large language models (MLLMs), the direct influence of the original image-text query can be weakened in the final retrieval representation. In this paper, we propose DCR-TSF, a training-free ZS-CIR framework that combines Dual-level Compositional Reasoning (DCR) and Triadic Spherical Fusion (TSF). DCR generates fine- and coarse-grained descriptions from the reference image and modification text, capturing complementary target-side semantics across different granularities. To preserve the original image-text query information, TSF constructs a composed-query anchor by spherically interpolating the reference image feature and modification text feature, and fuses it with the fine- and coarse-grained representations using a weighted Karcher mean on the unit hypersphere. The resulting final query representation balances detailed visual evidence, high-level semantic abstraction, and direct preservation of the original composed query. Extensive experiments on CIRCO, CIRR, and Fashion-IQ demonstrate that our method achieves state-of-the-art performance among training-free methods.

Abstract: Autonomous vehicles must detect objects faster than a frame camera delivers images. Event cameras record brightness changes with microsecond resolution, so RGB--event detectors can decide between frames, at an operating frequency, the rate of decisions, far above the frame rate. As this rate rises, each decision receives a sparser event window, and the reliability of the events relative to the frame varies over the scene and over time. Existing detectors weight the sensors per sample and encode the frame at the reduced event resolution. Reliability-supervised routing assigns a fusion weight to every location and supervises it with targets from the losses of two single-modality classifiers used only in training, moving loss-based supervision of fusion weights from whole samples to individual locations. Asymmetric-resolution fusion lets the frame carry the decision where the events fail: it encodes the frame at native resolution and pools every feature level onto the event grid, reusing all pretrained weights. On the DSEC-Det dataset, routing halves the accuracy loss from 20 to 180 Hz, from 6.3 to 3.1 mAP, and lowers it in every seed. The resulting detector, ERA-Det, sets a new state of the art at 63.2 mAP, 3.7 mAP above FlexEvent, leads at every operating frequency, and at 180 Hz is more accurate than FlexEvent at 20 Hz. On two DAVIS346 datasets, the router lowers the frame weight at night and in low light and raises it when the events vanish. ERA-Det upgrades released FlexEvent weights in 5,000 iterations on one GPU.

</details>

Abstract: Autoregressive (AR) video diffusion has emerged as a leading paradigm for efficient video generation, yet its practical deployment remains bottlenecked by the trade-off between generation quality and inference efficiency. We attribute this bottleneck to three error sources in AR rollouts: the base model error, the train-inference distribution mismatch, and the upstream error propagation. Building on this decomposition, we derive closed-form upper bounds on the per-step and cumulative errors, providing a principled basis for jointly optimizing generation quality and inference efficiency. Guided by this analysis, we propose TriTD, a Tri-Partite Trajectory-Distribution co-optimized distillation framework with three complementary modules. First, for ODE initialization, we adopt a Next-Node Trajectory Distillation (NTD) objective, which shortens the supervision horizon and admits a lower regression-error floor. Second, we introduce Chunk-Graded Noising (CGN), a monotonically non-decreasing per-chunk noising schedule that helps narrow the expected train-inference noise mismatch and removes redundant KV-cache updates, yielding substantial speedups at no quality cost. Third, we propose Orthogonal-Parallel DMD (OP-DMD), a distribution-matching loss that retains the original DMD supervision while introducing an additional regression constraint in the orthogonal subspace to suppress off-axis drift, improving both training stability and final generation quality. Extensive experiments show that TriTD consistently surpasses existing baselines in visual quality while reaching a real-time throughput of 26.8 FPS on a single H100 GPU, delivering a balanced quality-efficiency solution for interactive streaming video generation.

</details>

Abstract: Hierarchical world models plan over long horizons by passing predicted states from a coarse model to a lower-level controller. Choosing these states can be expensive: the upper-level planner repeatedly differentiates through candidate macro-action trajectories. We replace this search with a lightweight goal-conditioned network trained through the frozen high-level predictor. The network supplies a conditioning vector, while the predictor generates the next latent waypoint. Abstract: Graphical User Interface (GUI) agents increasingly act over streaming omni-modal observations spanning screenshots, audio, video, interaction history, and device states. However, exposing this complete context uniformly at every step ignores that relevance is transient and sparse: the signal determining one action can become clutter at the next. Because executed actions feed back into the context, these errors compound into Sequential Distraction, driving the agent into irreversible trajectory drift. We formulate the underlying requirement as Focused Perception: a representation that is step-adaptive, action-sufficient, and non-intrusive. Instead of uniformly processing all inputs, it compresses the most relevant evidence into a compact latent and subtly injects it into the frozen decoder's embeddings, guiding the model's attention without altering its native parameter space. Abstract: An effective visual tokenizer must preserve image fidelity while producing latents that downstream diffusion models can learn efficiently. Prior work improves latent diffusability through semantic alignment or joint tokenizer--diffusion training. Revisiting REPA-E, a representative approach, we find that removing its diffusion objective and dedicated generative blocks while retaining semantic prediction from corrupted latents yields comparable downstream generation performance under a matched setting. This observation motivates a general principle of robust prediction: generation-relevant information should remain recoverable from corrupted latents. We instantiate this principle with two complementary objectives. RobustAlign predicts pretrained semantic features from corrupted latents to preserve high-level structure, while RobustRecon reconstructs the clean image to retain fine-grained spatial and appearance information. Together, they form Robust Alignment and Reconstruction VAE (RoAR-VAE). Our analysis suggests that robust prediction improves diffusability by making useful information more accessible in high-variance latent directions, which diffusion models tend to learn earlier. Empirically, RoAR-VAE improves average generation benchmark performance by 2.19 points over the strongest baseline tokenizer under the same training budget, while improving small-text reconstruction accuracy by 16.93 percentage points over FLUX.1-VAE. These results demonstrate that RoAR-VAE achieves a favorable balance between reconstruction fidelity and downstream generative learnability.

Abstract: Diffusion transformers generate high-quality videos but remain computationally expensive. Sparse attention and temporal caching reduce computation within and across denoising steps, respectively, offering a natural composition. However, predictions produced with sparse attention become sources for temporal reuse, coupling their errors along the sampling trajectory. We observe that improving the accuracy of these sources recovers fidelity without changing the reuse schedule, and that sparsification errors remain directionally aligned across nearby steps, suggesting that correction information can be shared rather than recomputed. These observations motivate RECAP, a training-free method that couples sparse attention with hierarchical caching through reciprocal correction, adaptively coordinated by online error feedback. Cached dense refinement corrects omitted interactions in current sparse estimates, while fresh sparse statistics track input changes and regulate the aging correction. The resulting predictions then renew the sources for whole-step prediction reuse, allowing prediction history to advance between dense refreshes. We evaluate RECAP on multiple video diffusion models against sparse-attention methods, caching methods, and their direct compositions. On HunyuanVideo at 720p 5-second generation, RECAP achieves a 3.05x speedup and outperforms all tested direct compositions in both fidelity and inference speed.

Abstract: Autoregressive Diffusion Transformers have emerged as a powerful paradigm for long video generation. In this paper, we propose Dynamics-Aware Sparse Attention (DASA), a unified framework that shifts from dense computation to sparse, motion-driven generation to eliminate both storage and computational redundancies. Abstract: Video-Language Models (VLMs) excel at visual recognition but struggle with causal reasoning. Current evaluations are flawed: multiple-choice QA allows shortcuts, and open-ended QA lacks robust automatic evaluation. We introduce Causal Chain Generation (CCG), a novel task where models have to generate the sequence of intermediate events connecting a cause and an effect. This structured setup demands genuine reasoning and enables reliable automatic scoring. We accompany this task with two complementary metrics: SODA-Embedding, which measures the semantic matching of a generated chain against a reference, and Causal-QA, which probes the chain's causal coherence by asking whether it contains the specific causal facts needed to reconstruct the narrative. We provide two test sets: SF20K-CC, a manually annotated diagnostic on 20 short films, and CMD-CC, a movie clip benchmark derived from audio descriptions. Our analysis reveals that state-of-the-art VLMs struggle with narrative causality understanding, frequently hallucinating or omitting key events. To address this, we fine-tune VLMs with GRPO using a composite reward that combines a format reward, SODA-Embedding, and Causal-QA accuracy, demonstrating substantial improvements over strong baselines.

Abstract: Multi-drone collaborative detection aims to enhance target recognition by leveraging complementary observations from overlapping aerial viewpoints. Existing approaches typically follow a two-stage pipeline that first establishes cross-view correspondences and then performs fusion-based recognition. However, this decomposition suffers from inconsistent optimization objectives, redundant pairwise modeling, and irreversible error propagation, where incorrect associations inevitably undermine subsequent recognition performance. To overcome these limitations, we propose the Coupled Association-Recognition Network (CARNet), a unified framework that jointly optimizes cross-view target association and recognition fusion through a shared target-pair representation. To improve association reliability in densely populated scenes with visually similar targets, CARNet explicitly incorporates spatial cues into pairwise relation modeling. Moreover, we introduce two complementary causal-inspired intervention strategies: the first enhances cross-view association by emphasizing appearance-confusing pairs, while the second bolsters the robustness of fusion-based recognition by learning stable multi-view representations under incomplete visual evidence. In addition, we present MDMT-CD, a fine-grained extension of the MDMT dataset that enriches the original vehicle annotations with detailed category labels, providing the first benchmark specifically designed for multi-drone collaborative vehicle detection. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CARNet consistently outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods. Abstract: Post-hoc out-of-distribution detection usually relies on a single signal extracted from a frozen classifier, such as confidence, energy, features, or gradients. This dependence becomes fragile under classifier-level adversarial shift, where the shift can preserve the detector's chosen cue while changing the model's decision behavior. We investigate whether this failure mode can be mitigated by combining complementary detection criteria instead of selecting a single score. We introduce TriScore, a post-hoc OOD detector that combines three ID-calibrated signals from the same frozen backbone: an energy prior, a boundary-fragility score, and a transform-consistency score. These signals are standardized on ID validation data and fused by an input-dependent ID-residual gate, requiring no OOD labels, auxiliary model, training, or stored feature bank. Across four ImageNet-scale backbones, TriScore achieves the best mean AUROC among 15 post-hoc baselines under a non-adaptive adversarial-shift protocol, while remaining competitive on standard OOD benchmarks. Ablations show that the gains come from combining complementary cues rather than optimizing a single detector signal. These results support multi-criterion post-hoc detection as a practical route toward more robust OOD detection under classifier-level shift. The source code is publicly available at github.com/gursoyege/TriScore.

Abstract: Document understanding is increasingly judged on where an answer comes from, scored against annotated gold locations as if each fact lived in one place. In long visual documents, a fact often lives in several places: a chart value is restated in the text, a key figure reappears in the summary. We ask how often such copies occur in standard benchmarks and what they do to evaluation. We annotate 420 questions from MMLongBench-Doc and MMDocRAG with the locations that independently establish each required fact, checked by two independent reviewers. Copies are common and often cross-modal: 19.5% of MMLongBench-Doc test questions have a strict copy outside the gold, and in 28% of MMDocRAG questions a non-gold candidate alone supplies a required fact. Single-gold scoring then fails in three predictable ways. A retriever that finds a copy is scored as a miss: page recall@1 of text and visual retrievers is underestimated by 13-24 points on questions with a cross-page copy. A model that cites a copy is scored as citing wrongly: crediting equivalent evidence reverses 11% of pairwise orderings among 65 competitive MMDocRAG systems. Masking the gold region leaves the fact available elsewhere: with a copy in the input, 41-56% of correct answers across four models survive it yet fail once every annotated instance is masked. Set-level masking with automatically found copies closes 78% of the gap to masking every annotated instance at matched granularity (three models); an unverified key-string rule lowers survival at least as far but masks three times the area. We release the annotations and redundancy-aware metrics.

</details>
