Abstract: Referring Expression Comprehension (REC) localizes the image region described by a natural language expression, underpinning applications such as autonomous navigation, human-robot interaction, and medical image analysis. Under weak supervision, REC is challenged by the visual-linguistic semantic gap and the difficulty of inferring region-text correspondences from coarse image-level signals alone. Moreover, no single visual encoder captures all complementary properties---semantic alignment, fine-grained texture, and boundary awareness---while naively fusing multiple pretrained encoders yields heterogeneous, hard-to-reconcile representations. We propose LaMER, a weakly supervised REC framework that dynamically aggregates four frozen expert encoders through a language-conditioned Cross-Modal Routing Fusion module, adapting expert selection to each referring expression without instance-level bounding-box supervision. A Cell-Text Matching Head with Negative Anchor Augmentation, trained with a multi-objective loss combining contrastive, reconstruction, alignment, and load-balancing terms, further stabilizes optimization. LaMER achieves 77.67\%, 62.81\%, and 69.49\% Acc@IoU\(\geq 0.5\) on RefCOCO, RefCOCO+, and RefCOCOg, surpassing DViN and WeakMCN by up to 16.49 and 14.49 points, with ablations validating each component.

Abstract: Image-based spatial reasoning requires perception, decomposition, and inference, but the stage that limits accuracy can change during training. Answer-level rewards neither localize failures to individual stages nor provide discriminative group-relative feedback when all rollouts receive the same reward. Process supervision provides intermediate feedback, but fixed weights cannot adapt reward allocation to these changing bottlenecks. To address this gap, we propose CALIPER, an adaptive process-reward framework supported by SpatialTrace, a corpus of stage-decomposed spatial reasoning traces. CALIPER jointly estimates the associations between stage-wise reference coverage and answer correctness through within-group ridge regression, using these associations as utility proxies to gradually update reward weights. The resulting process reward supervises all rollout groups, including uniformly failing ones. Because utility estimates can become unreliable when answer-discriminative groups are scarce, a closed-loop harness monitors estimator reliability and validation performance to guide checkpoint rollback, rollout diversification, and early stopping. Extensive experiments across eight benchmarks and three backbones show that CALIPER achieves the highest mean under both one-shot and harness training, with consistent gains over fixed allocation across RL optimizers. Further analyses confirm the benefits of adaptive reward allocation and harness control, with favorable scaling in model/data size.

</details>

Abstract: While VideoLLMs have made significant progress in understanding short video benchmarks, they often struggle to summarize long-form ones. To tackle these issues, this paper introduces the first recursive Video Language Summarization Model (RVLSM) designed for video processing and tailored to long-form video-to-text summarization. It employs (1) Semantic Temporal Decomposition (STD), which preserves temporal coherence and continuity through a scene-aware chunking strategy that dynamically partitions videos according to semantic boundaries, and (2) Adaptive Multimodal Tool Calling (AMTC), which ensures semantic richness of the summary via a routing mechanism that selectively invokes auxiliary modules such as action recognition, optical character recognition, automatic speech recognition, and object detection. Experiments on the VideoXum and MixTyVSum benchmarks show that RVLSM consistently improves over zero-shot VideoLLM baselines across multiple model settings and performance metrics, and is competitive with a state-of-the-art fine-tuned model for video-to-text summarization. Abstract: Text-to-image diffusion models can produce unsafe content, while output filtering intervenes only after synthesis. We investigate whether native generation signals enable early detection of unsafe and adversarial inputs. We find that classifier-free guidance exposes strong safety signals in conditional responses, accessible through compact statistics and linear readouts. Based on this finding, we propose Conditional Semantic Trajectory Interception (CSTI), which aggregates activation contrasts between conditional and unconditional responses at early checkpoints for calibrated risk detection. CSTI fits concept-specific detectors using canonical concept and safe examples, without attack samples, intermediate image decoding, or generator modification. Across five generators, four adversarial attack collections, and two general NSFW datasets, CSTI achieves the strongest overall performance among six baselines at a matched 5% FPR. Abstract: Building upon our previous Ancilia framework, this work presents \emph{YUTELA}, an edge--server--cloud AIoT system for long-term, multi-site video anomaly detection. YUTELA replaces workstation-based on-site processing with NVIDIA Jetson Thor edge devices and redesigns the multi-camera pipeline through camera batching and redistribution, shared memory, reduced inter-stage data transfer, task integration, and TensorRT-accelerated inference. It maintains a separate anomaly model per camera, retrained on a centralized GPU server once enough recent pseudo-normal data are collected, with updated weights returned to the edge. With eight cameras, TensorRT increases throughput from 14.17 to 29.94 FPS under normal density, and the optimized edge node exceeds the throughput of the workstation-based Ancilia system in seven of nine workload settings, reaching up to 2.03 times its throughput at extreme density. This gain comes at the cost of higher latency under lighter loads with four or eight streams. YUTELA was then operated continuously for 170 hours at four public sites using 31 existing CCTV cameras. The deployment shows that runtime load is driven by scene activity and input frame rate rather than by camera count, and that retraining frequency varies by about three times across sites. The 101 automatically triggered training events occupied only about 6.6\% of a single GPU, indicating that one global node can support adaptation for tens of sites. Mean end-to-end alert latency ranged from 6.8 to 15.4~s depending on site traffic and anomaly type. These results show that YUTELA enables practical multi-camera edge processing with low-cost, camera-specific adaptation across heterogeneous sites.

Specific challenges arise due to the 3D volumetric nature of medical images, which translate to substantial demands in terms of memory, compute, and training data. Abstract: The quality of smartphone portrait photography has become increasingly important to both manufacturers and users. Recent devices use complex computational pipelines to improve facial rendering under varied lighting. These pipelines vary widely across manufacturers, so the same portrait can look noticeably different across smartphones. These differences affect multiple aspects of facial appearance, making perceptual preference difficult to characterize with a single measure. To address this, we propose an interpretable framework that decomposes face contrast into three components: overall facial tonal separation, eye-region contrast, and micro-contrast of fine skin details. We formulate dedicated metrics for each component and validate them via pairwise human judgments, demonstrating that our approach correlates more closely with human contrast perception than traditional contrast measures. To support this analysis, we introduce a realistic mannequin-based dataset designed for systematic facial contrast evaluation, comprising 508 full-resolution captures from 29 smartphones under diverse lighting, with region-specific masks and extensive pairwise perceptual annotations. As a downstream analysis, we show that combining the three component-wise metrics provides a meaningful basis for modeling balanced facial contrast preference in a controlled setting, and we present a preliminary evaluation on a smaller real-portrait dataset indicating that insights derived from the mannequin study can extend to real images. Our work provides a controlled first step toward defining and evaluating face contrast in portrait imaging.

We study these inference trajectories as a model-internal representation of realized reconstruction risk, measured by log-NMSE. Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) typically represent the world through latent embeddings from pretrained video models, which entangle heterogeneous aspects of the world, from how it looks (e.g., texture and color) to how it is physically configured (e.g., occupancy and pose). This raises an underexplored question: $\textbf{what should constitute the state of a WAM?}$ We argue that the world state should separate how the world appears from how the world evolves under action: the former captures semantic context, while the latter is governed by physical state. To this end, we propose $\textbf{PrismWAM}$, which decouples physics from semantics while jointly modeling state and action. PrismWAM factorizes an embodied task into a $\mathrm{Semantic\ Planning}\rightarrow\mathrm{Physical\ Rollout}\rightarrow\mathrm{Executable\ Action}$ pipeline: given only the initial RGB observation, semantic planning learns to predict the final goal image, while physical rollout predicts the fine-grained evolution toward this goal in depth space; executable actions are then generated from the depth rollout. Without any embodied action pretraining or additional data augmentation, PrismWAM achieves strong manipulation performance in both simulation and the real world. Notably, PrismWAM even surpasses a broad range of models with large-scale embodied action pretraining (e.g., OpenWAM-$α$). More broadly, we reframe World Action Models from modeling how the world looks to how it responds to action, paving the way for action-centric world models grounded in occupancy, flow, tactile sensing, proprioception, and beyond.

Second, a dual-scale condition-shift strategy performs global correction at low resolution, then refines the image at full resolution by shifting the denoising condition from the first-stage output to the original input for detail recovery. We also introduce DailyMoiré130, a benchmark of challenging moiré in everyday screen photography, comprising 130 smartphone captures across diverse displays with low-frequency color banding, fine high-frequency stripes, and mixed-frequency interference. Abstract: Composed Image Retrieval (CIR) retrieves products matching a reference image modified by natural language---a core capability for eCommerce search. Existing training-free methods fuse CLIP embeddings via arithmetic, but we identify a fundamental limitation: user text modifications are attribute-sparse---a user who types ``make it red with shorter sleeves'' specifies what to change but not what to keep (the floral pattern, the V-neck, the fit-and-flare silhouette). We introduce language-space fusion: a Vision-Language Model (VLM) jointly interprets the reference image and modification to generate a visually grounded product-title caption---e.g., ``Red floral fit-and-flare dress with short sleeves and V-neck''---that fills in the missing attributes from the image. Our method, CaptionFuse, requires no task-specific training and is model-agnostic. A controlled experiment confirms that attribute content--- not linguistic style---drives the gains: imperative and descriptive captions with identical attributes achieve equal R@10, while both outperform raw modifications by +6.5 points.

Abstract: Video face swapping transfers the reference identity to a target video while preserving its other attributes and motion. Despite recent progress in reference-guided flow-based methods, jointly achieving faithful identity transfer, high fidelity, and real-time generation over long video streams remains challenging. To address this challenge, we rethink the task-specific structure of reference-guided video face swapping. First, face swapping changes only identity while preserving most visual content, yet flow-matching supervision is weakly sensitive to these changes. We therefore develop Joint Rec-ID Loss, which balances explicit identity supervision and latent reconstruction according to the noise level. Second, the target video already provides non-edited attributes and temporal dynamics, enabling generation with fewer spatial tokens and shorter temporal contexts through Face-RoI Generation and Short-Context Rollout Training. By integrating these designs, we propose StreamingSwap, a reference-guided flow-matching framework for real-time streaming video face swapping. We also introduce HumanFaceVid, comprising 1,000 hours of cinematic training data and evaluation sets of 200 short and 200 long videos. Experiments demonstrate that our method achieves competitive identity transfer and visual quality at 20 FPS on a single NVIDIA RTX 4090 GPU, with stable generation over videos up to 10 minutes long. Abstract: Invisible watermarks are used to mark who owns a painting. We show that a simple attack removes the most robust one we test. The attack redraws the watermarked painting with brush strokes, using a pretrained stroke-based renderer. We call this attack BrushOff. It needs no training, no knowledge of the watermark, and no generative model. We test four watermarks on 500 paintings from five styles. VINE, a recent watermark built to survive image editing, survives all 14 conventional distortions we test, with at least 97.8% detection. Repainting lowers its detection to 1.8%, while the output keeps a no-reference quality score close to that of the clean paintings. Diffusion regeneration can also remove VINE, but at the same removal level it moves the image twice as far from the painting in DreamSim distance, and it changes what the painting shows. Because the renderer draws a countable number of strokes, we can control the attack strength step by step. Finally, we test what kind of signal survives. A simple probe that stores bits in the relative brightness of neighboring regions survives repainting, while a probe that uses absolute brightness does not. The same probe fails under diffusion regeneration, while VINE fails under repainting. Robustness to these two attacks is therefore a separate property, and benchmarks should test both.

</details>

Abstract: Recent video generation models produce visually compelling outputs, yet their physical plausibility remains poorly measured. Physical plausibility asks whether generated objects obey geometry, kinematics, dynamics, and conservation laws. Existing benchmarks rely on coarse visual-language model judgments or target embodied world models rather than video generation itself. We introduce PVBench, a physical video benchmark that quantitatively measures the plausibility of generated videos across 46 dimensions organized into seven domains, spanning: i) instruction and semantic fidelity, ii) behavioral and causal consistency, iii) perceptual and temporal quality, iv) geometric and spatial consistency, v) material properties and field states, vi) kinematic and spatiotemporal persistence, vii) and mechanical plausibility with conservation laws. PVBench comprises 600 carefully crafted prompts, of which 30% involve physical embodied agents such as robot arms, humanoids, quadrupeds, and multi-agent systems. For challenging physically tractable dimensions, it pairs each with specialized vision models that derive verifiable, physically grounded measurements, such as object-level 6D pose, metric scale, surface normals, kinematics, and numerical conservation-law residuals. It thus avoids relying solely on subjective scoring, while supporting both text-to-video and image-to-video generation. Finally, we validate the agreement between automatic metrics and human judgments through human evaluation. Meanwhile, we evaluate mainstream open-source and closed-source models and present several insights. By releasing the benchmark, prompts, and calibration protocol, we aim to foster physically grounded video generation.

</details>

Abstract: Reinforcement learning for instruction-guided image editing requires reward models that provide reliable, accurate, and efficient feedback. Direct scalar prediction compresses multidimensional editing preferences, whereas generative evaluators can expose their judgment through explicit reasoning. However, broad scoring criteria do not specify all instance-dependent editing and preservation constraints, and generating long reasoning trajectories is costly for online optimization. We introduce CheckDistill, a pointwise reward modeling framework that internalizes instruction-adaptive checklists. We decompose each editing condition into atomic, independently verifiable requirements and construct four-dimensional scoring labels together with structured verification evidence. After supervised score initialization, a privileged teacher conditions on checklists, item-level judgments, and dimension-level rationales to supervise the student's own scoring trajectories. A reliability-weighted on-policy self-distillation objective adjusts supervision at score tokens according to agreement between the teacher's expected scores and reference labels, while retaining candidate probability weights in a truncated token support. The deployed student directly predicts four score distributions without checklist inputs or explicit reasoning and aggregates their expectations into a scalar reward. CheckDistill achieves aggregate accuracies of 72.30%, 56.67% and 82.90% on MMRB2, MER-Bench and EditReward-Bench, respectively, exceeding the compared open-source baselines on all three metrics.

</details>

Paraphrasing leaves accuracy unchanged but flips 8--23\% of answers. Self-reported gains from agentic navigation and few-shot language priors cannot yet be attributed to language. Language thus adds real but narrow value, and we propose minimum controls that would expose these failure modes in future evaluations.

Abstract: Existing world models can generate action-conditioned video from a single image. Many focus on camera movement, allowing users to explore the generated scene. However, navigation alone does not ensure that an action follows persistent rules when its outcome depends on state that is invisible in the current frame. We present Stateful Harness, a framework that separates world evolution from video rendering to support stateful and rule-governed interaction. It maintains an evolving world state under specified rules, renders each resulting event with a frozen video model, and uses the generated clip to update the world state for the next turn. At each turn, the harness resolves the action selected by the user and compiles the resulting event into an instruction, while a preview clip from an editable 3D scene provides spatial and camera guidance. To evaluate rule compliance, we build WorldState-Bench, a benchmark of 50 counterfactual twin pairs that share an initial frame and action but differ in hidden state and required outcome. Under rule-only prompting, the best of the 11 evaluated video backends produces the required endings for both cases in 32% of pairs. For MiniMax-H3, specifying the required endings raises this rate from 32% to 64%, while the full harness reaches 92% with the same frozen model. These results support separating rule execution from video rendering for world interactions with hidden state.

Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) jointly predict future video and generate actions, yet remain sensitive to task-irrelevant visual perturbations, i.e., changes in visual appearance that should not affect robot behavior. We identify a gap in conventional WAM training: video prediction encourages representations to preserve appearance information, while action generation does not explicitly enforce invariance to task-irrelevant appearance. Data augmentation exposes WAMs to such perturbations but does not require perturbed and nominal observations to produce consistent action representations, and alignment with external pretrained features does not guarantee the desired invariance. To address this gap, we propose PICA-WAM, a self-supervised training framework that casts perturbation invariance as an auxiliary cross-appearance self-alignment task for WAMs. An exponential-moving-average (EMA) teacher processes the nominal observation to provide a deeper-layer target, while the online WAM processes a perturbed observation and aligns its shallower-layer action representation to this target through a lightweight projection head. Across four strong WAMs evaluated on RoboTwin 2.0, LIBERO-Plus, EBench, and a real robot, PICA-WAM consistently improves robustness under visual perturbations, preserves nominal performance in simulation, and raises the real robot success rate. PICA-WAM further reduces the sensitivity of video features to task-irrelevant appearance and its propagation into actions, as shown by paired layer-wise diagnostics. Code: https://github.com/YudeZou/PICA-WAM-release

Abstract: Robust scene understanding under varying environmental conditions is essential for safe autonomous driving. While cameras and LiDAR degrade in adverse weather and poor lighting, Frequency-Modulated Continuous-Wave (FMCW) spinning radar provides dependable 360° perception at extended ranges with minimal compute cost. However, real-time FMCW radar scene understanding is challenging due to complex signal returns, limited temporal resolution, and scarce annotated datasets. We introduce StreamRPS, a novel framework for streaming FMCW radar panoptic segmentation, comprising an automated label-generation pipeline via LiDAR scene completion and a real-time streaming network for semantic and panoptic segmentation. Our encoder introduces a stem to capture range-dependent signatures, anisotropic patch embeddings to preserve angular resolution, and axial polar attention to separately model the periodic azimuth and bounded range axes. We further introduce azimuth roll rotation augmentation to simulate ego-vehicle rotation during training. Comprehensive experiments on the SAX and BOREAS datasets validate both our auto-labeling methodology and streaming segmentation network against established baselines. We outperform the strongest baseline by at least 1.4pp while processing sectors at over 400 FPS in a 4-sector streaming setting. Abstract: A geometric style learned from one family of shapes is typically applied to new shapes from the same family. We consider a more challenging setting: learning the geometric design language of a stylized Latin alphabet and applying it to new forms, from Greek glyphs to entirely different structures such as human poses. We introduce Open-Form Geometric Style Transfer, where the forms to which a learned geometric style can be applied are not restricted to the shape family in which that style was observed. This setting is deceptively challenging: the difficulty is not merely learning a style, but learning the style without learning the shape family in which it was observed. We represent target forms by structural sketches and disentangle structural adherence from style realization using a Geometry LoRA and a style-specific LoRA. Critically, open-form transfer requires the Geometry LoRA to be universal. To achieve this, we devise a geometry-enrichment strategy that constructs a large and purposefully diverse collection of synthetic structural sketches spanning a broad range of unfamiliar geometries. Our experiments and ablations demonstrate the importance of both disentanglement and this universal geometry prior, enabling geometric styles learned from glyphs, pictograms, and other shape families to be transferred to substantially different, unseen forms.

Abstract: Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) achieve high-fidelity image and video generation, but they suffer from substantial inference costs due to repeated transformer evaluations across many denoising steps. Feature caching reduces this cost by reusing features across denoising steps. Recent forecasting methods extend this idea by extrapolating features to skipped steps instead of reusing them. However, existing forecasting methods extrapolate the features they predict uniformly, even though the spatial-frequency components of these features change most at different stages of denoising. They also overlook that denoising progresses nonuniformly, so equal step intervals can correspond to very different amounts of change. These mismatches amplify forecast errors as more steps are skipped, limiting the achievable acceleration. We propose SpecKCast, a training-free method that forecasts the model output in the frequency domain. SpecKCast applies a 2D discrete cosine transform (DCT) to the output and tracks each coefficient with a lightweight Kalman filter along a band-specific time coordinate derived from the noise schedule. The filter tracks each coefficient's value and rate of change, and the transformer is evaluated only when the filter's forecast uncertainty exceeds a threshold. Experiments on FLUX.1-dev, Qwen-Image, Wan2.1-14B, and HunyuanVideo show that SpecKCast achieves a better trade-off between acceleration and fidelity than existing caching and forecasting methods, reaching up to 6x speedup with less than 0.05 GiB of additional memory.

</details>

We introduce GenOlympiad, a benchmark which provides the most challenging text prompts from nine capability axes for stress-testing frontier text-to-image models. The assessed capabilities include generating large quantities, performing deep reasoning, ensuring hard physical consistency, following complex rules and so on. The prompts are designed such that prompt alignment is visually verifiable (e.g., counting) instead of relying on subjectiveness (e.g., style). We report that even the best-performing T2I model (Nano Banana Pro) only achieves a strict score of 23.9 out of 100 and that coding models such as GPT-6 Astra have higher prompt alignment scores (but not as visually pleasing). We present Ropedia W0, the first egocentric loco-manipulation world foundation model spanning humans and robots with diverse embodiments. We curate Xp-Data, comprising 10k hours of egocentric human data and 3k hours of robot data with unified multimodal annotations and systematic quality control. Abstract: Multimodal reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) provides reliable task-level supervision, yet offers limited guidance for localizing errors in visual reasoning. On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) complements this supervision with dense token-level corrections from a privileged teacher equipped with an evidence-centered visual view. However, the resulting corrections may conflate visual evidence with non-visual shifts induced by linguistic priors, response style, or the privileged view itself. We formalize this phenomenon as privileged-correction contamination: a token-level correction is contaminated when its update direction cannot be consistently attributed to the privileged visual evidence. Directly distilling contaminated corrections may therefore reinforce erroneous token-level updates. To address this issue, we propose reinforcement learning with visually attributable on-policy self-distillation, an evidence-consistent paradigm that augments task-level RLVR supervision with privileged token-level corrections grounded in visual evidence. We instantiate this paradigm with Visual-Attribution Gating (VAG), which uses the verifier-derived advantage as the optimization anchor and validates each privileged correction against the contrast between evidence-present and evidence-degraded teacher views. By admitting only corrections directionally supported by this visual contrast, VAG preserves visually grounded guidance while suppressing inconsistent residuals. Experiments across six visual reasoning benchmarks show that VAG consistently outperforms GRPO and OPSD methods, establishing new state-of-the-art average performance.

</details>

Abstract: Diffusion-based video matting has recently emerged as a leading approach, leveraging video diffusion priors from internet-scale pretraining to achieve strong zero-shot generalization from limited synthetic data. However, the heavy compute of these diffusion backbones limits inference to a short chunk of just 6 to 24 frames, so even ordinary videos span multiple chunks. Because each chunk is matted independently from coarse mask guidance, existing methods fail to maintain consistency across chunk boundaries, producing flickering edges and discontinuous transparency. We address this with a mask-guided diffusion-matting framework built on two ideas. The RGB and mask conditioning are represented as separate token streams that interact through full spatio-temporal self-attention, letting the model adaptively combine fine RGB texture with coarse mask layout rather than committing to a fixed channel-wise fusion. On top of this, we propose a novel pair of learnable embeddings, the preserve token and the refine token, that act as a per-frame conditioning interface and enable a sliding-window inference scheme in which already-generated mattes are propagated across chunk boundaries for local temporal consistency, complemented by a globally shared reference anchor at every chunk to prevent error accumulation in long sequences without any additional training. Across standard video matting benchmarks and in-the-wild long footage, our framework outperforms prior diffusion-based methods in both fine-detail accuracy and temporal consistency, and uniquely maintains consistency across chunk boundaries on long videos.

</details>

Abstract: While LLM agents can generate visually plausible CAD assemblies, these designs often fail to fulfill their intended physical functions. We study functional CAD design, a setting where agents interpret underspecified requests to construct and iteratively revise mechanisms that satisfy functional requirements in physical simulation. Verifying these requirements is nontrivial: different candidate mechanisms may realize the same function through entirely different components, controls, and operating sequences. To bridge this gap, we propose FunCAD-Harness, a framework that translates abstract requirements into candidate-specific physical tests and uses simulation feedback to guide design revisions. To systematically evaluate this capability, we introduce FunCADBench, a benchmark spanning 46 mechanism families with interdependent and multi-step operations. Experiments across frontier LLM agents show that FunCAD-Harness exposes subtle mechanical failures invisible to visual inspection, substantially improving functional success rates over standard simulation-augmented baselines. Ultimately, our work marks a step toward CAD agents capable of translating human intent into working mechanisms.

Abstract: Isochronous ultra-wideband (UWB) audio transport must deliver a complete service data unit (SDU) before its presentation deadline; fragment reception rate and aggregate throughput do not directly characterize this objective. We formulate admission and online service over a periodic calendar of finite exchange opportunities, explicitly accounting for fragmented SDUs, bounded attempts, calendar alignment, and timing uncertainty. For equal-duration, freely assignable opportunities, an interval-demand condition characterizes finite-job reservation feasibility, and an earliest-deadline-first construction realizes a feasible reservation. A separate necessary capacity bound identifies loads that cannot sustain complete-SDU delivery even with no transmission errors. For online overload control, we study a deadline-aware policy that discards an SDU when its outstanding fragments exceed the remaining eligible opportunities. Across 3,456 replicated scheduling runs and 432 supplemental sensitivity runs, pruning raises eight-stream burst-loss timely completion from 45.441\% to 59.399\%, a paired difference of $13.958\pm2.080$ percentage points (pointwise 95\% interval). At four streams, round robin instead attains slightly higher completion, while heavy overload causes pronounced per-stream imbalance. A finite design enumeration and an evaluated multi-stream fragmentation envelope quantify the interaction among scheduling, payload overhead, and capacity. These findings distinguish provable admission conditions from online deadline salvage and expose the aggregate-throughput versus per-stream-service tradeoff in UWB audio scheduling.

Abstract: Modern hearing aids combine noise reduction (NR) with wide dynamic range compression (WDRC) to suppress background interference while compensating for the reduced auditory dynamic range associated with hearing loss. Conventional systems typically process NR and WDRC as separate stages, although the output of NR directly affects the signal levels and envelopes that determine subsequent compression. Recent end-to-end neural approaches can jointly optimize these processes, but their dense computation remains challenging for always-on hearing aids. In this work, we propose SpikeAid, a causal end-to-end spiking neural network (SNN) framework for joint NR and WDRC. SpikeAid consists of an NR-Net that constructs complementary speech and noise representations and a WDRC-Net that estimates the corresponding branch gains, with both components jointly optimized according to the final hearing aid objective. To capture the multiscale temporal structures required by joint NR and WDRC in a computationally efficient manner, we further introduce the Delayed Resonate-and-fire Neuron (DRN), which incorporates bio-inspired heterogeneous dendritic delays and resonating dynamics. Experimental results on the hearing aid task show that SpikeAid improves key objective metrics over cascaded processing and outperforms existing spiking speech-processing baselines. Evaluation on the public Intel Neuromorphic Deep Noise Suppression (N-DNS) Challenge further demonstrates state-of-the-art neuromorphic speech-denoising performance with DRN. Finally, we designed an FPGA-based neuromorphic hardware system for efficient deployment of SpikeAid, where it achieves over two orders of magnitude higher energy efficiency than conventional CPU/GPU implementations, demonstrating its potential for energy-efficient hearing aid applications.

</details>

Abstract: LLM-based ASR systems often replace rare and out-of-vocabulary (OOV) words with familiar words that sound similar. Two factors contribute to this error: the model has not learned how an unseen word's sound maps to its spelling, and it may favor a familiar word over the one spoken, a failure we call prior suppression. Providing candidate pronunciations alone may not help: the model can treat a phoneme annotation as a marker that raises a candidate's output probability without checking its pronunciation against the speech. We propose PronCFT, which uses fixed phoneme sequences and trains the model to transcribe speech into phonemes, match them to context candidates, and output the matched spelling. During training, we alter candidate spellings while keeping their pronunciations unchanged, discouraging the model from choosing a familiar spelling without checking how the candidate sounds. A curriculum then gradually removes intermediate outputs to reduce decoding latency and prevent phoneme transcription errors from affecting the final text. The model's retained ability to use pronunciation cues suggests that this ability can be internalized. On an OOV test set filtered from open-source benchmarks, the final model, PronCFT-C, significantly improves recall from 59.89% to 83.57% (+23.68 pp). Wrong-phoneme and spelling-interference tests indicate that the gain depends on phoneme content rather than the presence of an annotation.

However, existing benchmarks cover limited ranges of specific multi-speaker conditions and scenarios, making it difficult to reveal system limitations under diverse conditions and scenarios. We introduce MS-Bench\footnote{\href{https://aslp-lab.github.io/MS-Bench/}{Demo page: https://aslp-lab.github.io/MS-Bench/}}, a condition-stratified benchmark for fine-grained MSASR evaluation. MS-Bench integrates both public and internal benchmarks and characterizes each recording along five dimensions: speaker number, overlap ratio, speaker similarity, speaker turn interval, and acoustic quality. We stratify these dimensions into condition ranges and construct MS-Bench with broad coverage of conditions, scenarios, languages, and recording devices. Evaluating representative commercial, open-source, and cascaded systems, we find that overlap affects both lexical recognition and speaker attribution, while other factors exhibit more task-specific effects on recognition, attribution, or temporal prediction. These results demonstrate that aggregate metrics alone obscure important MSASR failure modes and highlight the value of condition-stratified evaluation. MS-Bench aims to reveal condition-specific limitations of current MSASR systems and guide future improvements.

Abstract: Dilution of precision is the standard way to describe navigation geometry and it does not apply in cislunar space: its derivation presumes a snapshot solution in which simultaneous measurements determine the state at one instant. A spacecraft tracked by inter-satellite ranging in the Earth-Moon three-body problem has no such solution: range is a function of position only, so the velocity columns of the measurement Jacobian vanish identically and the state is unobservable however many links are in view. The correct figure of merit is dynamical, because the three-body flow carries velocity information into later range measurements. We demonstrate this on four differential-corrected planar distant retrograde orbits, reporting the observability Gramian rather than a dilution of precision. Instantaneous range-only geometry has rank 2 of 4; the full state becomes observable only after a tracking arc of 2.09 h, the first sampled arc at full rank on a 0.26 h grid, and a square-root information filter recomputing the same quantity by a different route becomes well-posed at exactly that arc. Beyond it, conditioning improves monotonically with arc length, the Gramian condition number falling from 1.2e11 at 3 h to 1.4e5 at 48 h. Adding range rate lifts the instantaneous rank from 2 to 4, so a Doppler-capable link makes a snapshot solution exist where a range-only link cannot. Lifting the known-reference assumption, the joint problem of estimating a station and the satellites' states and clocks together is rank-deficient by exactly one without an external interferometric observable in all twenty realizations tested, and full rank with it; the deficiency persists only to four satellites. For the configurations studied, cislunar navigation requirements are better written as arc-length, conditioning and measurement-type budgets than as dilution-of-precision thresholds, which are undefined here.

</details>

Abstract: This paper presents sequence-to-sequence (seq2seq) neural surrogates for high-speed transient response modeling and signal integrity analysis in fixed and variable design parameter spaces. To identify the structural requirements for prediction accuracy, multiple neural architectures were evaluated with respect to different training strategies such as sample-based and unit interval-based, as well as input configurations such as TX-only and TX+RX. For fixed design parameters, feedforward neural networks achieve accuracy comparable to recurrent models while requiring lower computational cost, as the static mapping can be approximated by fully connected and non-sequential representations. Variable design parameters alter the electromagnetic characteristics of the interconnect, which creates parameter-dependent temporal dynamics that require sequence-aware architectures to resolve the evolving interactions within the transient response. The results based on a 14-dimensional variable design parameter scenario show that while autoregressive TX+RX feedback suffers from severe phase drift and error propagation under variable conditions, non-autoregressive TX-only inference leads to stability. To overcome the structural limitations of standard decoders, a recurrent Early Fusion LSTM is proposed, which injects design parameters at the initial encoding stage. This approach achieves superior prediction accuracy, with an RMSE of 31.0 mV and an R2 of 99.51% for transient waveform prediction, an RMSE of 30.7 mV and an R2 of 99.57% for eye-height prediction, and an RMSE of 19.5 ps and an R2 of 99.94% for eye-width prediction. These results demonstrate that neural seq2seq surrogates are accurate for transient response modeling and signal integrity analysis in complex variable design parameter spaces.

Implanted microscopic transmitters and receivers can exchange information using molecules that diffuse through a biological medium, enabling them to form a network and jointly coordinate sensing and actuation operations. However, randomness in molecular propagation causes severe signal distortion, and receivers have limited computational capacity to compensate. The receiver design must therefore combine robustness to diffusion-induced distortion with computational architectures amenable to implementation in the chemical domain. We study the task of synchronizing the receiver's clock with a sequence transmitted through a molecular communication (MC) channel. The physics part approximates the maximum-likelihood function synchronization objective, while the other learns a timing policy using this objective as its reward. Simulation results yield clock misalignments above half a second, compatible with molecular transmissions operating on the timescale of seconds.

Abstract: High-frequency (HF) skywave radars require high gain at elevation angles of 3 degrees to 30 degrees, where vertically-polarized monopoles over lossy land suffer from pseudo-Brewster cancellation. This letter shows that curving a long monopole creates a horizontally-polarized radiation channel much less affected by this cancellation. Closed-form bounds derived from the space-wave field over a homogeneous half-space show that the vertical-current field is suppressed below an elevation set by the soil permittivity, whereas the horizontally-polarized field of an elevated arc is nearly unaffected by the soil. Bayesian optimization (BO) of an analytical model, followed by 182 full-wave HFSS simulations with analytical Fresnel far-field post-processing, was used to design the element at 15 MHz. Over moist soil, a 15-m element with an 86 degrees arc is near-resonant (-25-dB match), 93% efficient, and 25% lower than a straight element of equal length. It raises the azimuth-averaged 3 degrees-30 degrees gain by 2.5 dB and, on every land ground considered, exceeds the 0.575λ straight monopole that is optimal over seawater and PEC by 1.0-2.6 dB.

Abstract: Integrated sensing and communication (ISAC) is envisioned as a cornerstone technology for next-generation networks, yet it introduces significant security risks in both communication and sensing domains. To mitigate the coverage limitations inherent in conventional mono-static or bi-static setups, this paper proposes a networked ISAC framework empowered by movable antennas (MAs). By leveraging flexible antenna reconfiguration, MAs provide additional spatial degrees of freedom to significantly boost beamforming gain and interference suppression. Specifically, we investigate a novel dual-domain security paradigm where networked base stations collaboratively protect uplink (UL) and downlink (DL) transmissions from communication eavesdropping while simultaneously safeguarding target sensing data against sensing eavesdroppers (SEs). To this end, we formulate a joint optimization problem to maximize the sum of secrecy rates, subject to sensing requirements, SE eavesdropping probability constraint, and practical MA deployment constraints, by jointly optimizing receive combiners, transmit beamformers, UL power allocation, and MA positions. To address this non-convex problem with highly coupled variables, an efficient alternating optimization algorithm is proposed, integrating closed-form solutions for receive combiners, successive convex approximation for transmit beamforming and UL power, and a tailored sensing-aware multi-candidate particle swarm optimization for the high-dimensional MA positioning. Extensive numerical results validate the effectiveness of the proposed algorithms, demonstrating that the proposed MA-enabled networked cooperative ISAC design significantly outperforms conventional fixed-position antenna-based designs in terms of security performance, while achieving notable power- and antenna-saving gains.

Abstract: Maternal and perinatal deaths are concentrated in low- and middle-income countries, where routine fetal monitoring is largely inaccessible. With the prevalence of smartphone as personal assistive gadget, we ask whether a phone's built-in microphone, with no extra hardware or emitted signal, can serve as a software-only maternal-fetal cardiac screening aid when moved from the chest to the lower abdomen? We report a longitudinal, self-recorded case study of a single subject (Z) over the last five weeks of a singleton pregnancy, later delivered at term without complications: 28 short, uncontrolled recordings (rest, morning, post-exercise). Lacking maternal/fetal ground truth, we exploit the prior that maternal and fetal heart rates occupy largely disjoint frequency bands, blindly decomposing a Shannon-energy envelope into two candidate-rate bands, and additionally probe a slower breathing-rate band as a rest/exercise discriminator. A fast, regular, fetal-plausible rhythm was recovered from most recordings and remained essentially unchanged across maternal activity states, consistent with normal fetal cardiac autonomy; a slower, noisier maternal-plausible rhythm was recovered less reliably. Beat-to-beat variability proved largely noise-dominated at our short window lengths, and the breathing-rate probe did not significantly separate rest from exercise; both are reported as negative findings. We position the work within AI for global health: a low-cost screening aid for remote and underserved communities, pending reference-validated follow-up, not a replacement for clinical monitoring.

Abstract: The beam squint effect has traditionally been viewed as detrimental to near-field wideband beamforming due to the reduction of beamforming gain towards the desired direction. In this paper, we show that it can instead be harnessed as a benefit in a hardware-constrained orthogonal frequency division multiple access (OFDMA) system, where the system, equipped with a single radio-frequency (RF) chain, aims to serve multiple spatially distributed users by allocating different subcarriers to different users. By exploiting the beam squint effect, flexible frequency-dependent beamfocusing patterns are realized via parallel true-time-delay (TTD) units, enabling nearly full beamforming gain across users. In particular, we formulate a max-min rate optimization problem that jointly designs the time delays, subcarrier-user association, analog beamforming, and power allocation. To tackle the resulting nonconvex mixed-integer problem, a two-stage optimization framework is proposed. In Stage~I, subcarrier-user association and structured analog beamforming are jointly optimized. In Stage~II, power allocation is obtained via bisection search and inverse water-filling. Simulation results demonstrate that the proposed method can achieve a significant improvement by utilizing beam squint to serve multiple spatially distributed users in OFDMA systems.

</details>

This paper presents Fluxion, a Rust audio DSP runtime for cascaded second-order sections, FIR filters, and delay-line effects. Fluxion implements the parts that are specific to DSP: closed-form filter design, forward kernels, analytic adjoints for the recursion, and stability checks. One graph algebra is lowered to a differentiable training engine and to an allocation-free real-time engine; between them, a trained graph is checked for stability and frozen into a versioned artifact that an edge device checks again before playback. We compare Fluxion with six framework-hosted and native baselines under one float32 protocol. Among the systems that compute the exact recursion, Fluxion has the highest GPU throughput when data stays on the device and the highest throughput on most CPU workloads, while its CPU training step is slower than those of torchaudio and GRAFX. Its test suite checks the audio thread for allocations with an instrumented allocator, and we report measured latency tails. Fluxion is open source at \url{https://github.com/matteospanio/fluxion}.

Abstract: Electroencephalographic (EEG) functional connectivity is quantified using diverse measures of statistical dependence, spectral coupling, phase synchronization, information transfer, directed interactions, cross-frequency coupling, and network topology. An important question is what can be inferred about one connectivity measure from another. This paper develops a mathematical framework that treats connectivity measures as mappings from an explicitly defined admissible state space to observable values. For a source measure and a target measure, the set of target values compatible with an observed source value is defined as a cross-connectivity attainable set. This formulation distinguishes exact determination, conditional determination using additional information, bounded inference, approximate identification, and non-identifiability. The framework is extended to multichannel connectivity matrices, graph measures, and dynamic connectivity. It also incorporates the influence of filtering, referencing, volume conduction, source leakage, inverse modeling, epoch duration, and estimator assumptions on admissible inferences. Analytical examples examine relationships between covariance and correlation, mutual information and correlation under joint Gaussianity, coherence and phase-locking value under restrictive signal conditions, and the general non-identifiability of the phase-lag index and weighted phase-lag index without additional assumptions. By making assumptions and attainable values explicit, the framework provides a systematic basis for determining what connectivity results can and cannot establish about other measures.

Abstract: The wide footprint of low-Earth-orbit (LEO) satellite downlinks exposes the mere occurrence of a transmission to any receiver within it. This paper studies covert multiuser LEO downlinks, where a passive warden at an unknown but fixed ground location accumulates observations as the satellite moves. We jointly optimize the precoding and the occurrence probabilities of the multiuser symbol combinations to provide covertness against such wardens. Each phase-shift keying (PSK) symbol vector is decomposed into a relative pattern and a common rotation, so that shaping the pattern probabilities leaves the symbols of every legitimate user uniformly distributed. We develop probabilistically shaped symbol-level precoding and probabilistically shaped block-level precoding. We derive the exact chi-square divergence of the warden's Gaussian-mixture observation over the complete session and, by bounding the leakage over a continuous location uncertainty region, obtain certificates that guarantee covertness throughout that region. The certified covertness level is minimized under robust constructive-interference, power, and entropy constraints through an alternating optimization whose updates are equivalent to convex conic problems, and the optimized probabilities follow a Maxwell--Boltzmann-type law. Numerical results show that shaping substantially reduces the certified covertness level of both precoding structures, particularly under large location uncertainty, low elevation, and scarce power.

Abstract: The need for user-specific calibration remains an often-cited barrier to widespread adoption of myoelectric control. This study compared six calibration-free controllers, trained on 306 users of the EMG-EPN-612 dataset and used without any calibration data from the test participant, against three calibrated controllers in a closed-loop Fitts' law target-acquisition task with sixteen participants. The calibrated controllers achieved higher offline balanced accuracy (90.6% against 73.1% for the best controller of each paradigm), but completion rates did not differ significantly between paradigms, and the highest completion rate was achieved by a calibration-free controller. Calibrated controllers moved faster on successful acquisitions, partly because of their individually fitted speed mappings, though not significantly after correction for multiple comparisons. However, every calibration-free controller produced more direct and stable trajectories than every calibrated controller, even after adjusting for cursor speed. Offline accuracy did not predict completion rate across controllers or within participants, and across paradigms higher offline accuracy was associated with poorer trajectory quality in 15 of 16 participants. Without any calibration data from the end user, a model trained across a large population matched calibrated control in completion rate and exceeded it in trajectory quality. The offline accuracy gained through calibration should therefore not be taken as evidence of a closed-loop benefit.

Abstract: This paper considers a cell-free (CF) network under active eavesdropping. Abstract: Affine frequency division multiplexing (AFDM) is a promising waveform for future high-mobility communications due to its Doppler robustness and diversity gain. However, existing continuous-time AFDM relies on chirp-dependent wideband receive filtering, which increases noise power and causes channel-energy leakage. In this paper, we propose an AFDM reception scheme based on pre-filter de-chirp for doubly selective channels. The coupling between de-chirp and the channel is first characterized, yielding an equivalent path representation with delay-coupled effective Doppler shifts. The discrete affine Fourier transform (DAFT)-domain equivalent channel is then derived under general transmit pulse shaping and linear time-invariant receive filtering, showing that the effective filtering kernel depends only on the relative sample offset and avoids the additional spreading caused by conventional chirp-dependent filtering. The receive-noise power is further analyzed, and an explicit expression for the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) gap is obtained by the required receive-filter bandwidths. Simulations verify the theoretical SNR analysis and show that the proposed scheme significantly outperforms the conventional scheme while approaching ideal discrete-time AFDM.

Abstract: Accurate radio-based indoor localization remains challenging in multipath-rich and dynamic indoor environments. Supervised fingerprinting methods achieve high positioning accuracy by learning mappings from radio channel measurements to physical locations, but require costly position labels and frequent recalibration. Channel Charting (CC) addresses this limitation by learning low-dimensional representations of Channel State Information (CSI) without position labels, yielding channel charts in which nearby points correspond to nearby physical locations. However, prominent CC methods rely on setup-specific dissimilarity metrics, such as ADP-, CIRA-, or PDR-based distances, and on precomputed $O(n^2)$ distance matrices, limiting scalability and transferability across radio systems. We propose ChronoChart, a timestamp-supervised CC framework that requires only CSI and timestamps without prior knowledge for training. Our method jointly optimizes complementary objectives: a triplet loss using temporally derived positive and negative samples to establish coarse global structure, a temporal-proximity loss that promotes smooth trajectories in the chart, and a joint-embedding loss that enforces consistency between representations of different augmentations. Evaluated on real-world 5G, MIMO, and passive UWB data, our method matches or exceeds prior CC baselines that depend on tailored distance metrics, and remains scalable and applicable across heterogeneous radio systems.

</details>

Abstract: Reliable radio-based indoor localization is essential for industrial automation, yet existing methods are often evaluated in environments that do not fully reflect the severe multipath and non-line-of-sight (NLOS) conditions encountered in real deployments. This limits our understanding of how AI, i.e., modern geometric, unsupervised, and semi-supervised localization methods generalize to industrial 5G settings. We present RAIL-5G, a public real-world 5G NR channel impulse response (CIR) benchmark recorded in an operational railway maintenance facility. RAIL-5G covers an 811m$^2$ indoor area with eight distributed radio units, robot-mounted user equipment (UE) trajectories, high-accuracy iGPS reference positions, and severe metal-induced multipath caused by train compartments and maintenance infrastructure. We benchmark geometric time-difference of arrival (TDoA), supervised fingerprinting, unsupervised channel charting, and self-supervised wireless pretraining methods. Our results highlight the difficulty of RAIL-5G: on the full dataset, TDoA reaches 7.02m mean average error (MAE), the best unsupervised method reaches 2.46m, and supervised fingerprinting achieves 0.52m. Self-supervised pretraining reduces the labeling effort by up to one order of magnitude, but the remaining gap to supervised learning shows that robust AI-based localization under severe multipath remains an open challenge. The dataset and code are available at https://iis.fraunhofer.de/rail-5g.

Abstract: In this paper, we study the sampling of bandlimited signals on multigraphs, where several graphs with different shift operators are defined on the same set of nodes. Since the shift operators do not commute in general, they admit only a joint block diagonalization, and the frequencies of the multigraph signal model are matrices rather than scalars. Relying on a formal algebraic convolutional signal model, we introduce a notion of frequency and bandwidth for multigraph signals that does not depend on any individual layer, and we extend the notions of removable and uniqueness sets to multigraphs. We prove that the optimality of a sampling set is determined exclusively by the common Fourier basis and its invariant subspaces, and not by any individual layer, although the individual layers provide computable sufficient conditions that certify a sampling set as a uniqueness set. Since identifying which layers provide reliable certificates requires spectral information, we propose an aggregate operator that pools the information of all the layers, is computable directly from the shift operators, and certifies uniqueness without any assumption on the individual layers. This operator leads to a greedy sampling algorithm that does not require computing the joint block diagonalization. Numerical experiments on multigraphs whose layers order the frequencies inconsistently show that the proposed algorithm selects better-conditioned sampling sets and attains lower reconstruction errors than single-layer methods.

</details>

Abstract: Coherent distributed radar architectures enhance the detectability of low-reflectivity targets. However, conventional coherent processing generally entails prohibitively large volumes of inter-station data exchange, thereby motivating the adoption of one-bit quantization strategies. In this work, we investigate a clutter-dominated regime in which the interference is driven by a dense ensemble of weak scatterers. We demonstrate that, under these conditions, one-bit quantization can exhibit a vanishing performance loss with respect to ideal high-resolution sampling in terms of detection capability, in contrast to the classical 2 dB performance penalty associated with one-bit detection in white Gaussian noise environments. For unknown target velocity, we reformulate joint velocity-search processing under the effective one-bit model. Maximizing over the velocity plane makes false-alarm calibration an extreme-value problem for a correlated two-dimensional random field, whose maximum is not characterized by its pointwise null distribution alone. We therefore use an expected Euler characteristic approximation to calibrate the search threshold. We obtain analytic expressions for a separable two-station geometry and use constrained coefficient fitting for larger networks. Monte Carlo simulations demonstrate negligible quantization loss in the clutter-dominated setting and conservative false-alarm calibration under the tested Gaussian slow-time model.

Abstract: Time-interleaved analog-to-digital converters (TI-ADCs) enable multi-gigasample-per-second throughput in high-speed applications, but sub-picosecond timing skews among interleaved channels introduce large spectral artifacts that degrade dynamic performance. To continuously track parametric drifts without interrupting normal operation, background calibration is required. However, extracting timing skews under unknown inputs is challenging, and many existing correlation-based methods rely on resource-heavy derivative filters that restrict input bandwidth. This article proposes a novel background timing-skew estimation technique for two-channel TI-ADCs that overcomes these limitations. Abstract: Single-photon avalanche diode (SPAD) arrays offer photon-level sensitivity for sensing in optical integrated sensing and communication (O-ISAC). However, after each detection, a SPAD is blind for a dead time, which saturates the array at high received power. Existing O-ISAC analyses assume linear receivers and do not capture this effect. In this paper, we study ranging with a SPAD array in a monostatic O-ISAC system based on direct-current-biased optical orthogonal frequency-division multiplexing (DCO-OFDM). Sampling the array once per dead time, we model the number of pixels that detect a photon in each sampling interval as a binomial random variable whose success probability accounts for the intensity variation of the echo within a dead time. Based on this model, we derive the Cramér-Rao bound (CRB) for range estimation. We show that the SPAD count model of optical wireless communication, which assumes a constant photon rate over a dead time, causes a range bias that depends on the received power. Moreover, unlike for a linear photodiode, saturation degrades the ranging accuracy beyond a sensing-optimal transmit power. The rate-constrained operating point is then the larger of this power and the power needed for the target data rate. Numerical results validate the analysis against a continuous-time photon-counting simulation and show that the proposed model is needed for ranging.

</details>

Abstract: GNSS signal acquisition is the process of detecting and estimating signal parameters by exploiting correlation operations that provide processing gain and enable the detection of signals below the noise floor. The design of an acquisition engine requires balancing achievable sensitivity, computational complexity, memory requirements, power consumption, and implementation complexity. In particular, increasing coherent and non-coherent integration times improves sensitivity but can significantly increase receiver processing requirements, limiting its applicability in low-power and resource-constrained receivers.
  This paper presents a comparative analysis of L1-band signal acquisition complexity by evaluating the required integration time and corresponding implementation requirements for different signal designs under equivalent acquisition performance targets. The analysis considers the impact of signal characteristics, including spreading codes, secondary codes, modulation, and signal bandwidth, on coherent and non-coherent acquisition performance. For a given attenuation level and target probability of false alarm and detection, the required integration times are derived and translated into acquisition complexity metrics, including arithmetic operations, processing time, and memory requirements.
  The analysis is applied to Pulsar X1, a LEO-enabled PNT signal, and representative GNSS L1 signals to determine under which attenuation, assistance, and integration conditions the higher received power of LEO-PNT translates into reduced receiver acquisition workload. By relating the required integration to arithmetic operations, processing time, and memory requirements, the study provides a receiver-level comparison relevant to both conventional and snapshot-based receivers, where reducing acquisition processing can directly contribute to lower energy consumption.

Abstract: Most existing studies on pinching-antenna systems optimize the antenna positions or activation states under a prescribed waveguide deployment, where mutually parallel waveguides are commonly adopted. However, it remains unclear whether, and under what conditions, such a deployment is optimal from a communication-performance perspective. To fill this gap, this paper investigates long-term waveguide deployment optimization for pinching-antenna systems over a rectangular service region. We first consider the fundamental two-waveguide case and derive a closed-form sufficient condition, jointly determined by the waveguide height and the service-region aspect ratio, under which a deployment parallel to the longer side of the service region is globally optimal. We then consider the general multi-waveguide case, where the orientation and offset of each waveguide are jointly optimized without prescribing any deployment pattern, and develop an adaptive exchange algorithm for the resulting nonconvex optimization problem. We further show that, within the parallel class, the waveguides should be aligned with the longer side of the service region, while in the large-height regime, the globally optimal unrestricted deployment asymptotically concentrates all waveguides on the centerline along the longer side. Numerical results validate the proposed algorithm and reveal how the preferred deployment geometry depends on the waveguide height and service-region shape. In particular, nonparallel deployments can provide noticeable gains in intermediate-height regimes, whereas parallel or center-concentrated deployments become favorable in other regimes.

</details>

Abstract: We develop a vision--radio-frequency (RF) cooperative integrated sensing and communication (ISAC) framework that jointly uses visual priors and RF observations for transmit-side beam control and receiver-side clutter suppression. Within a narrowband snapshot model, visual target priors and historical RF detections guide control in dynamic vehicle-to-infrastructure networks with roadside clutter. In this framework, posterior angular uncertainty determines beam control and the selection between communication-for-sensing and sensing-for-sensing modes. To reduce sensing overhead while accommodating unreliable visual information, we develop a reliability-adaptive beam scanning scheme that allocates a bounded scan budget according to visual target probabilities and RF history, while reserving an adaptive fraction for exploration. At the receiver, visual geometry identifies clutter support, and RF observations estimate the corresponding complex reflection coefficients. Motion-aware residual filtering is then applied before angle--Doppler detection to suppress clutter while preserving slow-moving targets. Furthermore, communication and sensing power allocation is formulated as a per-direction linear program, subject to signal-to-interference-plus-noise ratio constraints evaluated at angles sampled from the posterior distribution and a total-power budget. Simulation results show that the proposed framework improves tracking over static and vision-only beam control and reduces sensing overhead relative to full scanning. The proposed scanning scheme also provides greater robustness to visual blockage than fixed-weight scanning, while the receiver better preserves slow-moving targets than temporal cancellation when visual target information is available.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence (AI) is increasingly applied to 5G/6G network optimization, yet differences in empirical evidence, evaluation methods, and computational requirements complicate comparisons of reported performance. This scop ing review synthesizes 72 peer-reviewed journal studies pub lished between 2022 and 2026, selected through a PRISMA ScR-informed process from 893 records. The review examines empirical tools, AI/ML techniques, optimization domains, and operational readiness. Evidence classification identifies 37 studies (51.4%) using real-world or physical-testbed evidence only, 25 (34.7%) combining measured and simulated evidence, and 10 (13.9%) relying exclusively on synthetic or simulated data. Offline analysis was the most common evaluation setting, accounting for 45 studies (62.5%). Three principal findings emerge. First, empirical measurements improve contextual relevance, while simulation and digital twins support scalable experimentation but introduce validation challenges. Second, algorithmic effectiveness depends on the optimization objective, data characteristics, and computational constraints rather than model complexity alone. Third, predictive accuracy is more consistently evaluated than the network-level effects of optimization decisions. The review there fore introduces the six-module Evidence Fidelity and Operational Reporting (EFOR) checklist to improve evaluation consistency, reporting transparency, and operational relevance.

</details>

Abstract: Phase modulated continuous wave (PMCW) radar is an attractive choice for automotive sensing due to its high dynamic range, interference robustness, and support for integrated communication and sensing. In multiple-input multiple-output (MIMO) PMCW radar, code division multiplexing (CDM) separates transmit signals, but non-ideal auto- and cross-correlation properties increase range sidelobes and degrade dynamic range. This paper proposes a Doppler-compensated space-time coding (STC)-based MIMO PMCW radar framework that separates transmit signals before range correlation, eliminating cross-correlation terms under perfect compensation and allowing the same binary sequence to be used across transmit antennas. The Doppler influence on STC decoding is analytically characterized, showing that chip-wise and inter-STC-slot phase distortions increase range sidelobes and generate ghost components in the direction-of-arrival (DOA) spectrum. To mitigate these effects, Doppler is estimated first, followed by joint fast-time and STC-slot compensation before decoding. Residual STC leakage under imperfect Doppler estimation is quantified, and the processing is extended to multiple moving targets through Doppler-bin-dependent compensation. Theoretical analysis and simulation results demonstrate reduced range sidelobes and suppression of DOA ghosts, while maintaining flexibility in code sequence design.

Abstract: We introduce Long Listening Thoughts (LLT), a framework for constructing post-training datasets that improves audio reasoning by pairing deliberate perceptual grounding with explicit cognitive behaviors. On audio understanding and reasoning benchmarks, large audio-language models (LALMs) are limited primarily by perceptual errors, yet existing post-training datasets rely on audio descriptions of limited diversity. Through a four-stage pipeline, LLT (1) builds multi-aspect dense audio descriptions spanning semantic, acoustic, and temporal information, (2) generates more challenging questions, (3) synthesizes incorrect reasoning traces, and (4) extends short reasoning traces into long ones via a thought-continuation strategy that elicits cognitive behaviors such as verification and backtracking. Using LLT, we synthesize 1.6M reasoning traces and train Qwen2.5-Omni-7B with a two-stage pipeline of supervised fine-tuning (SFT) followed by direct preference optimization (DPO). This improves accuracy on MMAU Test-mini (71.5 to 78.2), MMAR (56.7 to 63.7), and MMSU (60.6 to 66.0).

Abstract: Conditional-flow text-to-speech (TTS) models can achieve high synthesis quality with relatively few sampling steps, but collapsing the remaining trajectory to a single update can still cause substantial quality degradation. We propose MixiSpeech, a progressive state distillation framework that converts a multi-step conditional-flow acoustic model into a one-step generator while preserving its architecture and speaker conditioning. Rather than training the one-step model directly, MixiSpeech progressively reduces the global sampling budget through a 10-4-2-1 curriculum, with each student trained to match local teacher-integration endpoints using a masked state-matching objective. We instantiate the framework on a 710-speaker Vietnamese Matcha-TTS model trained from scratch on PhoAudiobook. On 100 shared test text-speaker pairs, direct one-step inference substantially degrades synthesis quality, obtaining 1.6846 UTMOS22-Strong and 6.21% WER compared with 2.2923 and 4.65% for the 10-step teacher. In contrast, the distilled one-step model achieves 2.2678 UTMOS22-Strong, 4.63% WER, and 2.83% CER, recovering most of the quality lost at the one-step operating point while requiring only a single acoustic vector-field evaluation. End-to-end inference time decreases from 0.0662 s for the 10-step teacher to 0.0312 s for MixiSpeech, corresponding to a 2.12 measured speedup. These results show that progressive state distillation can make one-step conditional-flow TTS practical without relying on a large multi-step sampling budget. Project page: https://mixispeech.solanai.us/

</details>

Abstract: Real-time voice cloning has enabled large-scale streaming deepfake and vishing attacks, while existing Network Detection and Response (NDR) solutions rely on GPU-based post-processing that requires buffered audio and generalizes poorly to unseen speech synthesis models. This paper presents SENTRY, a hardware-oriented, streaming trust engine for inline deepfake detection at the network edge. SENTRY employs an integer lifting-wavelet front end together with a bank of multiplier-free transforms, including wavelet-packet envelopes, Walsh-Hadamard sequency profiles, waveform micro-statistics, and long-term spectral features, designed so that acoustic analysis needs only integer add, shift, compare and bitwise operations. A supervised gradient-boosted tree classifier is combined with an unsupervised Mahalanobis out-of-distribution detector trained solely on authentic speech, and their outputs are fused by a lightweight logistic layer to produce graduated LOG, CHALLENGE, or DROP actions. Under a closed-set protocol, SENTRY reaches 98.75% accuracy at a 1.25% equal-error rate (EER) on WaveFake, with perfect separation of HiFi-GAN and WaveGlow; its supervised track alone achieves an AUC of 0.9994 (fused: 0.9993). On ASVspoof 2019 LA (attacks A01-A06) over an 8 kHz mu-law telephony channel, it reaches 94.75% accuracy at a 5.05% EER, with a supervised-track AUC of 0.9948 (fused: 0.9884) and per-attack EERs below 7%. In our prototype, the feature front end runs as bit-exact integer code on one Arm Cortex-A53 core, and the classifier and fusion stage are synthesized for a Xilinx Zynq UltraScale+ MPSoC using no DSP slices, returning a decision 220 ns after a feature vector is ready. These results indicate that deepfake screening can be placed inline without GPUs or floating-point hardware; generalization to unseen generators is left to future work.

</details>

Abstract: Automatic Speech recognition (ASR) struggles with Pathological speech due to persistent speaker-specific mismatch. In clinical and accessibility scenarios, only limited target-speaker data is typically available, making full model retraining impractical. We present a component-level analysis of LoRA adaptation in Whisper, investigating how adapter placement interacts with the model size, training efficiency and utterance-duration in single-speaker settings. Our experimental comparative study shows that while scaling reduces errors, it does not eliminate mismatch. Furthermore, the full attention LoRa provides the most reliable accuracy, and the best time--accuracy trade-off. Improvements vary across utterance length, with medium-length recordings benefiting the most, while short and long utterances remain challenging. These findings highlight the importance of adaptation placement and duration-dependent evaluation, under challenging, data-scarce personalization scenarios.

Abstract: Audio fingerprinting methods identify audio based on its content, even when the content has been possibly corrupted. Despite their applicability and usage in the speech domain, research and advancements in audio fingerprinting traditionally focus on music and are almost exclusively evaluated on music. We provide insights into speech-oriented audio fingerprinting by benchmarking the best performing audio fingerprinting models on speech data while taking into consideration the effects of noise, reverberation, temporal changes, pitch modifications, and lossy compression. Our findings indicate that audio fingerprinting methods based on self-supervised learning outperform classical approaches on speech data, while the performance of these models on speech is inferior compared to the performance that they achieve on music. Results also include an analysis of false positives, showing that fingerprinting methods are more likely to match with an audio of the same speaker rather than an audio of the same text, when mismatches occur.
