Abstract: Few-shot audio classification requires audio--language models to distinguish variable acoustic events from limited labeled data. PALM's main formulation adapts encoded class representations without changing pretrained encoders, but its class representations remain input-independent and classification uses audio features without class-conditioned refinement. We introduce DF-PALM, a dynamic feature-space prompting framework that combines audio-conditioned class representations with asymmetric cross-attention for audio refinement. Class representations are conditioned after text encoding, held fixed across fusion layers for each input, and reused for final scoring. Both pretrained encoders remain frozen, avoiding audio-conditioned text-encoder passes. With PENGI as the frozen backbone, the main 16-shot evaluation covered 11 benchmarks and six baselines. DF-PALM achieved 80.69% mean accuracy and 80.32% macro-averaged F1, exceeding PALM by 4.11 and 4.30 percentage points, respectively. Paired benchmark-level tests supported both gains after Holm correction. Across four shot settings, DF-PALM achieved the highest mean accuracy and macro-averaged F1 among the four evaluated prompt-learning methods. The gains extended to LAION-CLAP and OpenFLAM, with significant improvements over PALM in both metrics. At 50 candidate classes, operator-counted inference floating-point operations increased by 0.39% relative to PALM. These results support instance-conditioned feature-space adaptation as an effective approach to few-shot audio classification. Our code is publicly available at GitHub https://github.com/sunyulin0421/DF-PALM.

</details>

Abstract: Automatic video dubbing (AVD) requires temporally aligned speech, transcripts, and video, yet existing corpora vary in audio quality, transcription reliability, and speaking-face visibility. We introduce VoxDub, a bilingual lip-synchronized audio-visual dataset containing 766K clips and approximately 1.58K hours of in-the-wild Chinese and English recordings, covering varied speakers, scenes, speaking styles, and linguistic content. Its curation pipeline combines speech-centered segmentation with dual-ASR transcription checking, speech quality filtering, face visibility checks, and lip-speech synchronization verification. Text-to-speech (TTS) experiments show that training on VoxDub yields lower word error rates and higher speaker similarity than training on the compared audio-visual corpora, LRS3 and CN-CVS. AVD experiments show broadly comparable speech quality and subjective synchronization. These results support the utility of VoxDub for TTS and AVD training. More details are available on the project website (https://voxdub-dataset.github.io/VoxDub/).

Abstract: Preference optimization promises to improve text-to-speech (TTS) systems by learning which speech outputs are better. External expert speech seems a natural source of preferred responses, especially when student generations contain errors. Yet demonstration quality does not establish whether the student can benefit from that supervision, or whether better target fitting will produce better speech. We examine this gap across two TTS students using real and synthetic expert speech, comparing preference objectives and direct versus bridge supervision while tracking fixed-sequence likelihoods and generation metrics. With expert supervision held fixed, objectives differ markedly in their sensitivity to preference-score scaling and the resulting generation quality. Likelihood diagnostics characterize these responses, but cannot establish their benefit: expert likelihood can increase while intelligibility worsens, or decrease while it improves. Bridge supervision highlights a distinction between mitigating degradation and improving the student: it can recover performance lost under direct training without consistently surpassing the pretrained base. Route benefits vary across objectives, students, and expert sources; neither route consistently dominates. Together, these findings distinguish demonstration quality, target fitting, and generated-speech quality as complementary criteria for assessing expert supervision.

</details>

Abstract: Audio-visual deepfake detectors are often trained under a single dataset or manipulation regime, but multi-source training collections may contain heterogeneous attacks and uneven source distributions. Training separate detectors for each source is inefficient, while a single shared detector may be biased toward dominant source-specific patterns. We study known-source imbalanced multi-source audio-visual deepfake detection using FakeAVCeleb and LAV-DF, where source identity is supplied during training and inference. To address this setting, we build on the Multi-Scale Cross-Modal Transformer Encoder (MSCT) and propose Pre-MSCT Shared-Private Feature Fusion. Our method routes \texttt{[CLS]}-augmented audio and visual tokens into a shared stream and source-specific private streams before the first MSCT block, while retaining its internal multi-scale self-attention (MSSA), differential cross-modal attention (DCA), and feed-forward operations. The shared stream models cross-source reusable evidence, while the private streams retain source-dependent residual variations. A domain-aware gated residual fusion module injects selected private evidence into the shared representation for the main fused prediction. Experiments under two controlled source-composition protocols show improved macro and worst-source AUC compared with the MRDF and MSCT baselines. These results support shared-private cross-modal discrepancy learning in the evaluated two-source setting with known source identity.

</details>

Abstract: Code-based agents can operate creative software, but mixing requests often describe an intended sound without specifying the edits needed to achieve it. We present TOMORI, a code-based mixing-agent harness for native digital audio workstation (DAW) control. Custom plugins provide REAPER project inspection, optional audio measurement, and execution of model-generated Lua code. All observations return as text; delivery includes an editable project and rendered audio. TOMORI-BENCH pairs 110 human-reviewed reference edits with three instruction forms and evaluates native DAW execution end to end through operation structure, parameter precision, and rendered-audio outcomes. Across 990 cases from three primary models, explicit instructions yield high reference agreement, while abstract requests reduce project agreement and audio improvement. Paired ablations show that the full workflow improves explicit execution over a single submission. Prompted audio measurement succeeds in all 28 selected abstract cases but yields no clear overall improvement on the reference-based metrics and increases tool calls. These results expose the gap between executable DAW control and precise realization of perceptual mixing goals.

Abstract: People look up a song by humming it, but also by whistling it or singing it with the wrong words. Published query-by-humming systems and benchmarks focus on hums and sung queries, and no public dataset pairs whistles or wrong-lyrics singing with song recordings. We study both query types with a melody-only retrieval system: a key-normalized pitch track embedded by a 5.2M-parameter contour encoder, with a spectral-peak tracker for whistles. First, a physics-grounded generator renders synthetic whistles and nonsense-syllable singing from the vocal melodies of 11,083 songs. Training on them raises top-1 retrieval of held-out synthetic queries against an 11,050-song gallery from 0.180 to 0.251 for whistles and from 0.193 to 0.264 for wrong-lyrics singing, but the gain does not transfer: on the MLEnd test performers the best synthetic-only model gets 210 of 339 real whistles right at top-1, against 214 for the baseline. Second, a closed-set fine-tune on real MLEnd whistles with performer-disjoint splits raises whistle-to-hum top-1 from 0.631 (95% CI 0.581-0.681) to 0.673 (0.622-0.723), on known songs only. Third, two further generations of synthetic queries also fail to transfer: melody-conditioned whistles and la-la singing from a commercial neural music model leave real-whistle top-1 at 0.631, and an open-source pipeline (YingMusic-Singer-Plus singing with partly wrong, mumbled or nonsense lyrics, a DDSP whistle synthesizer, and a melody-error model fitted on real performers) gives at best 0.619 (paired difference -0.012, 95% CI -0.038 to +0.015). Across physics-based, commercial neural and open-source neural synthetic data, nothing transferred to real whistles, which points to collecting real queries as the missing step. Code is public; models and data will be released.

Abstract: Large self-supervised speech models provide powerful representations for speaker verification, but full fine-tuning is expensive. Most conventional adapters insert small trainable modules to modify the output of each layer, while keeping the original residual connections unchanged. Building on our preliminary study of manifold-constrained Hyper-Connections in speaker encoders, we explore parameter-efficient adaptation of both residual paths and feature selection across layers. We first propose a Manifold-Constrained Residual Adapter (MCRA), which splits the hidden dimensions of each Transformer residual state into N groups and uses a small Sinkhorn-normalized matrix to exchange information among them. A learnable gate controls the balance between the original and mixed residual paths. The gate adds only one scalar parameter at each residual connection and initially favors the original path, allowing gradual adaptation. We further propose Frame-Adaptive Layer Aggregation (FALA), an attention-based aggregation method that uses a single learned query shared across all utterances to compute layer weights separately for each speech frame. Because the layer representations depend on the input, the resulting weights vary across both utterances and frames, unlike the fixed weights of a conventional layer-weighted sum. Compared with a frozen WavLM baseline using a fixed layer-weighted sum, our method achieves relative EER reductions of 22.4% on VoxCeleb1-O and 2.9% on CN-Celeb, with trainable adaptation parameters equivalent to only 0.0009% and 0.0016% of the WavLM-Large parameters, respectively.

</details>

Abstract: Answering machine detection (AMD) is a fundamental task in telephony that classifies whether an answered call is picked up by a human or a voicemail (machine). Despite its widespread use across cloud communication platforms, existing approaches rely on proprietary systems with undisclosed architectures, or require computationally expensive models that cannot operate in real time. We propose a lightweight AMD system that combines pretrained audio embeddings with gradient boosting machines, trained to prioritize the correct detection of human-answered calls. We evaluate seven models across two datasets: three models that we propose, a proprietary industry model, two prior academic approaches, and a large language model baseline that relies on automatic speech recognition. Furthermore, we release OpenAMD, the first publicly available AMD dataset comprising of more than 500 recordings in 7 languages. Our best model achieves the highest precision across both datasets while maintaining low inference latency. We assess the robustness of models to additive noise and packet loss, and perform tests of statistical significance to discover per-language and per-gender model performance. Finally, we evaluate our model on an out-of-domain task of detecting robocalls, where it achieves 94.34% recall without any additional training, which is comparable to the industry standard.

</details>

Abstract: Music structure analysis (MSA) aims to identify functional labels and their boundaries in music. Despite recent progress, three challenges remain. First, fine-grained annotations for MSA are scarce. Second, existing systems often share one encoder between boundary detection and functional labeling despite their different information requirements. Third, most end-to-end systems are audio-only and ignore structural cues from lyrics. To address these issues, we construct SheetSage-MSA, an MSA dataset derived from SheetSage, containing 13,880 cleaned excerpt annotations from 9,010 songs and approximately 82.5 hours of annotated audio. We further propose SCALE (Structural Context from Audio and Lyric Evidence), which uses separate task-specific audio pathways to model local transitions and long-range structural context, and incorporates timestamped lyrics and lyrical recurrence to refine chorus predictions. On the HarmonixSet benchmark, SCALE outperforms the existing state-of-the-art SongFormer by 3.5 and 4.2 percentage points in HR.5F and HR3F, respectively, while improving ACC by 0.9 points. An interactive demo page containing the source code is also available at https://multimodal-music-research-lab.github.io/SCALE/.

Abstract: Large Audio Language Models (LALMs) reason over speech, environmental sounds, music, and other acoustic information. However, their audio encoders produce dense token sequences that make long-form audio costly to process. Existing audio pruning methods reduce this cost using heuristics such as attention, representation similarity, or temporal sampling, but do not explicitly learn which tokens are most useful for a given question. We propose AudioScorer, a lightweight model that scores audio tokens according to the user question. It is trained by distilling the ranking of audio tokens under the frozen LALM's answer-gradient saliency, together with downstream answer supervision. Compared with other pruning methods, AudioScorer achieves the best or tied-best result at 10% token retention on 7 of 10 semantic and acoustic tasks, including speech recognition, emotion recognition, and audio-scene classification.

</details>

Abstract: Ground truth in Speech Emotion Recognition (SER) is often presented as a single categorical fact, yet an acted emotion and the emotion heard by listeners describe different aspects of the same recording. We examine this distinction in Emozionalmente, an acted Italian corpus that provides both an elicited production label and five listener judgements for every clip. Using frozen XLS-R, HuBERT, and WavLM representations with matched probes, we compare three label authorities: the expressed condition, the listeners' hard plurality, and their complete vote distribution. The results tell a consistent story. Recovering the expressed condition is easier than predicting a tie-broken listener plurality, but that gap compares two different questions rather than revealing a general loss of SER ability. As listener agreement weakens, the plurality becomes an increasingly brittle hard target. Training on the full vote distribution does not substantially change which class wins the model's hard decision, but it produces probability assignments that more closely follow the alternatives selected by listeners, especially for ambiguous clips. No authority therefore replaces the others. Expressed labels describe how speech was produced, pluralities provide an operational single-class decision, and vote distributions preserve perceptual uncertainty. For human-facing Internet of Sounds applications, evaluations should state which authority is being predicted, retain its aggregation rule and agreement information, and use distributional scores when disagreement is part of the target.

</details>

Abstract: A cover song re-renders an existing piece, most commonly by preserving its tonal content -- melody and chord progression -- while reshaping other musical attributes. This suggests a natural formulation for generative cover synthesis with pretrained music language models: explicitly control tonal content, while specifying lyrics and style through text. Existing approaches condition on frame-level features, tightly coupling generation to the reference's timing and structure, and limiting flexibility such as tempo variation, structural rearrangement, or partial conditioning. To this end, we present MuLaCover, a cover generation model that conditions a pretrained text-to-song foundation model on an editable symbolic lead sheet. Our design enables alignment-free control: generated outputs preserve the tonal signature of the source without frame-level alignment, allowing flexible timing, structure, and segment-level generation. We further introduce a training curriculum with partially mismatched audio-symbolic pairs, improving diversity and robustness. We evaluate MuLaCover with objective metrics, standard subjective ratings, and in-depth expert interviews that provide fine-grained diagnostic insights. Experiments show that MuLaCover achieves state-of-the-art performance among open-source systems and is competitive with leading commercial models such as Suno-v5.5. Audio samples and code are available at our project page: https://mulalabs.ai/mulacover/

</details>
